- Official syllabus 2025 -
- Consolidated data - India's Relations with Neighbors -
Neighbor Major Issues Neighbor's Strategies Against India India's Counter Policies Key Developments (2025)
Afghanistan
  • Taliban Governance: Post-2021 Taliban takeover, lack of inclusive government, and human rights concerns, particularly for women and minorities, strain India's diplomatic engagement.
  • Cross-Border Terrorism: Tehrik-i-Taliban Pakistan (TTP) and other groups operating from Afghan soil, with 30% rise in attacks in 2025, threatening India's security in Jammu & Kashmir.
  • Refugee Influx: Over 500,000 Afghan refugees in India, with 20,000 new arrivals in 2025, creating humanitarian and security challenges.
  • Water Disputes: Kabul River water sharing concerns, as Pakistan-backed projects reduce downstream flow to India’s Indus system.
  • Economic Isolation: Trade routes disrupted via Pakistan, limiting India’s $800M trade potential; drug trafficking from Afghanistan ($500M annually) impacts India.
  • Geopolitical Competition: Chinese and Pakistani influence in Kabul undermines India’s historical ties.
  • Pakistan-Taliban Alliance: Pakistan's support for Taliban provides safe havens for anti-India groups like TTP, escalating border threats via PoK.
  • China’s BRI Extension: $10B mining deals (Aynak copper, lithium) and CPEC extension to Afghanistan, aiming to marginalize India’s Chabahar route.
  • Anti-India Propaganda: Taliban media portrays India as anti-Muslim, leveraging OIC platforms to criticize India’s Kashmir policy.
  • Regional Encirclement: China’s investments in Wakhan Corridor to connect with CPEC, creating a strategic arc against India.
  • Terror Financing: Drug and arms networks in Afghanistan indirectly fund anti-India militants, with $200M traced in 2025.
  • Humanitarian Aid: $3B in aid since 2001, including wheat, medicines, and schools, to maintain soft power and support Afghan civilians.
  • Chabahar Port: $1B investment to bypass Pakistan, enabling trade and aid delivery via Iran, strengthening India’s Central Asia access.
  • Neighborhood First Policy: Engaging interim Afghan government for counter-terrorism, bypassing Pakistan’s influence.
  • QUAD and IPEF: Leveraging US, Japan, Australia to counter China’s influence in Afghanistan through development projects.
  • Counter-Terrorism: Intelligence sharing with interim government; support for anti-TTP operations via regional forums like SCO’s RATS.
  • Cultural Diplomacy: Promoting historical ties via Ariana heritage projects and scholarships for 1,000 Afghan students annually.
  • Aid Resumption: $50M aid package in 2025, including 20,000 MT wheat and medical supplies, delivered via Chabahar.
  • TTP Attacks Surge: 30% increase in border incidents; India raised concerns at UNSC counter-terrorism meet.
  • Chabahar Trade: $800M bilateral trade maintained; new logistics hub operational in 2025.
  • India-Hosted Talks: Hosted Afghan civil society talks in Delhi, pushing for inclusive governance.
  • China’s Mining Deals: India countered with $100M renewable energy offer to Kabul interim government.
Pakistan
  • Territorial Disputes: Ongoing conflict over Jammu and Kashmir, worsened by the 2019 abrogation of Article 370, leading to diplomatic standoffs and border skirmishes.
  • Cross-Border Terrorism: Frequent attacks by groups like Lashkar-e-Taiba (LeT) and Jaish-e-Mohammed (JeM), with over 40 incidents in 2025, including the Pahalgam attack killing 28 civilians.
  • Water Sharing Conflicts: Violations of the Indus Waters Treaty (IWT), with Pakistan’s suspension post-2025 Pahalgam attack, raising concerns over water security.
  • Maritime Disputes: Sir Creek boundary issue, hindering maritime cooperation and resource exploration in the Arabian Sea.
  • Trade and Economic Barriers: High tariffs and non-tariff barriers restricting bilateral trade to $2.4 billion, despite potential in textiles and agriculture.
  • Nuclear Risks: Escalation risks due to Pakistan’s full-spectrum deterrence doctrine, including tactical nuclear weapons to counter India’s conventional strength.
  • CPEC (China-Pakistan Economic Corridor): $60B+ Belt and Road Initiative through Pakistan-occupied Kashmir (PoK), challenging India’s sovereignty and enabling Chinese military presence, with 2025 extensions to Afghanistan.
  • Proxy Terrorism: State-backed support for militant groups like LeT and JeM, responsible for 40+ attacks in 2025, to destabilize India’s security and global image.
  • Internationalization of Kashmir: Raising Kashmir at UNSC and OIC, portraying India as an aggressor to isolate it diplomatically.
  • China Alliance: Deepening military and economic ties with China, including joint exercises and technology transfers, to create a two-front threat against India.
  • Global Advocacy: Leveraging UN and OIC to challenge India’s actions in Kashmir, pushing for plebiscites and human rights probes.
  • Neighborhood First Policy: Prioritizing peaceful ties but suspending SAARC summits due to terrorism, focusing on bilateral engagement.
  • De-Hyphenation Strategy: Engaging South Asian neighbors separately from Pakistan, strengthening BIMSTEC to bypass Pakistan’s veto.
  • IWT Enforcement: Using treaty suspension as leverage post-attacks, seeking arbitration via Permanent Court of Arbitration.
  • FATF Pressure: Ensuring Pakistan’s 2025 grey-listing to curb terror financing, limiting its economic maneuverability.
  • QUAD and IPEF: Building alliances with US, Japan, Australia for maritime exercises in Arabian Sea, countering BRI/CPEC.
  • Surgical Strikes: Operation Sindoor (May 2025) neutralized terror camps, signaling zero-tolerance for cross-border threats.
  • 2025 Crisis: May airstrikes, drones, and cyber attacks post-Pahalgam; 40 civilian deaths claimed by Pakistan; ceasefire reaffirmed but fragile.
  • Trade Suspension: Attari-Wagah trade halted temporarily, dropping to $2.4B; Afghan trucks allowed in May.
  • US Mediation: India rejected Pakistan’s UNSC complaint but engaged in US backchannel talks; MNNA status revoked.
  • Aid Pause: $100M aid withheld; focus on countering CPEC-Afghanistan extension.
  • Diplomatic Gains: FATF grey-listing extended; India hosted BIMSTEC security dialogue excluding Pakistan.
China
  • Border Disputes: Standoffs in eastern Ladakh and Arunachal Pradesh, with 2025 patrols resuming post-2020 Galwan clash (20 Indian deaths).
  • Galwan Legacy: Incomplete disengagement, with 30,000 troops deployed; buffer zones restrict patrolling.
  • Trade Imbalance: $135B total trade in 2025, with $100B deficit; critical minerals access restricted.
  • Water Security: China’s Yarlung Zangbo dams (e.g., Medog mega-dam) threaten downstream Brahmaputra flow; no binding data-sharing pact.
  • South China Sea Influence: China’s IOR expansion impacts India’s maritime interests in Maldives and Sri Lanka.
  • Technology Espionage: Over 500 apps banned due to Huawei, TikTok data risks.
  • String of Pearls: Strategic ports in Gwadar (Pakistan), Hambantota (Sri Lanka), Kyaukpyu (Myanmar), Payra (Bangladesh) to encircle India.
  • BRI Expansion: $1T global initiative, $60B in South Asia; 2025 Afghanistan extension via CPEC.
  • Salami Slicing: Incremental territorial grabs in Ladakh (Depsang plains) to alter status quo.
  • Five Fingers of Tibet: Claiming Nepal, Bhutan, Sikkim, Arunachal to encircle India; 2025 Sakteng claim.
  • Debt-Trap Diplomacy: Loans to Nepal ($10B, Pokhara), Sri Lanka ($8B, Hambantota), Maldives ($1.4B).
  • Global Forums: Blocking India’s CCIT at UN; SCO push for BRI.
  • Act East Policy: Strengthening ASEAN ties ($90B trade) to counter China’s SE Asia dominance via EAS, ADMM+.
  • Necklace of Diamonds: Chabahar (Iran), Duqm (Oman), Sittwe (Myanmar) ports to secure IOR access.
  • Project Mausam: Cultural-historical IOR links with 30+ countries for soft power.
  • De-Hyphenation: Engaging neighbors independently; $1B Teesta counter to China.
  • QUAD and IPEF: Strategic-economic bloc with US, Japan, Australia; 2025 IMEC launch.
  • Cheque Book Diplomacy: $4B to Sri Lanka, $2,150 Cr to Bhutan to offset China.
  • Technology Controls: App bans, semiconductor self-reliance.
  • Border Talks: Wang Yi’s August visit; Brahmaputra data shared; Depsang patrols resumed.
  • Trade Surge: $135B total, $100B deficit; $2B critical minerals deals eased.
  • Yarlung Zangbo Dam: India protested Medog dam; flood risk concerns raised.
  • SCO Tensions: India opposed CPEC; QUAD joint exercise hosted.
  • Visa Normalization: Tourist visas resumed; 24th SR dialogue held.
Bangladesh
  • Water Sharing: Teesta river talks stalled since 2011, impacting 30M people; 2025 interim pact on 6 rivers excludes Teesta.
  • Border Security: 50 killings in 2025 (up from 40 in 2024); smuggling and cattle trade disputes persist.
  • Rohingya Crisis: 1M+ refugees, 150K new arrivals in 2025; radicalization risks in camps.
  • Trade Barriers: Non-tariff issues (standards, ports) limit $16B trade; garment exports dominate.
  • Enclave Issues: 2015 LBA exchanges incomplete; citizenship delays for 14,000 people.
  • Radicalization: Islamist groups gain traction post-Hasina ouster.
  • BRI Investments: $40B in Payra, Matarbari ports; anti-India Islamist media support.
  • Teesta Project: China’s $1B water management offer to undermine India.
  • Golden Friendship: Military exercises with China; anti-India alignment post-Hasina.
  • Rohingya Militants: Indirect support for ARSA to destabilize borders.
  • Media Campaigns: Anti-India narrative on Rohingya, water issues.
  • Neighborhood First: SAFTA duty-free access for $16B trade; $120 Cr aid.
  • BBIN MVA: Regional transport to boost connectivity, bypassing Pakistan.
  • Kushiyara Agreement: 2022 water-sharing interim for Kushiyara river.
  • 6-River Interim Pact: 2025 agreement for Manu, Muhuri, etc., to build trust.
  • Maitree Power Plant: 1320 MW coal plant for energy security.
  • BrahMos Offer: Missile sales to counter China; anti-radicalization training.
  • CEPA Progress: Negotiations advanced; $1B Teesta counter-offer to China.
  • Yunus-Modi Meet: BIMSTEC summit on extradition, Teesta; $500M aid sought.
  • Rohingya Deportation: Stalled due to Myanmar coup; India hosts 40K refugees.
  • China Oil Refinery: $1.4B deal countered with India housing/rail projects.
  • Trade Growth: $16B; BrahMos deal signed in 2025.
Nepal
  • Territorial Disputes: Kalapani, Limpiyadhura, Lipulekh; 2025 map row with China escalates tensions.
  • 1950 Treaty Review: Nepal seeks revision; open border exploited for smuggling.
  • Hydropower Dependency: 70% revenue from India projects; environmental concerns raised.
  • Susta Enclave: Unresolved exchange; Chinese encroachments in Humla.
  • Border Blockades: 2015 blockade legacy; 2025 trucker strikes disrupt trade.
  • Nationalism Surge: Oli’s pro-China tilt fuels anti-India protests.
  • BRI Debt-Trap: $10B investments; Pokhara airport ($200M loan, over 50% debt).
  • Three-Step Roadmap: 2021 border deal with China; Sakteng claim escalates.
  • Anti-India Media: Nationalist campaigns on Kalapani, backed by China.
  • Teesta Counter: $1B China offer to undermine India’s water diplomacy.
  • Foreign Influence: Chinese military aid; Russian arms deals to Nepal.
  • Neighborhood First: $700 Cr aid; 4,000 housing units funded.
  • 1950 Treaty: Basis for open borders; hydropower cooperation.
  • Arun-3 Hydropower: $1.5B project; 900 MW capacity.
  • IBC Ties: International Buddhist Conclave for cultural soft power.
  • De-Hyphenation: Bilateral aid bypassing China; Shree Airlines support.
  • Lipulekh Protest: India-China deal denounced; Oli-Modi UNGA meet on border.
  • Aid Surge: $700 Cr; hydropower contracts prioritized over China.
  • Kalapani Row: Map dispute persists; $8B trade, $5B Indian exports.
  • Shree Airlines: China flights resumed; India counters with aviation aid.
  • Border Talks: 25th round stalled; trucker strikes resolved with India mediation.
Bhutan
  • Doklam Tri-Junction: 2017 standoff legacy; 2025 patrols resumed amid tensions.
  • Chinese Encroachments: 22 villages built, 764 sq km claimed; Sakteng sanctuary dispute.
  • Hydropower Dependency: 70% revenue from India projects; environmental concerns.
  • Buffer State Erosion: Chinese settlements in Beyul Khenpajong/Menchuma.
  • Jakurlung/Pasamlung Swap: China’s territory exchange proposal raises concerns.
  • BRI Investments: Dorjee Khandu airport; debt-trap fears intensify.
  • Three-Step Roadmap: 1998-2021 border deal with China; nationalism leveraged.
  • Sakteng Claim: Chinese map inclusion; anti-India protests sparked.
  • Foreign Influence: Chinese military camps near Doklam.
  • Encirclement: Part of China’s Five Fingers strategy.
  • Neighborhood First: $2,150 Cr aid, largest recipient.
  • Hydropower Treaties: Punatsangchhu (1,020 MW), Mangdechhu (720 MW).
  • Gelephu Mindfulness City: $1B support for sustainable urban project.
  • Doklam Status Quo: 1998 agreement enforcement; joint patrols.
  • De-Hyphenation: Bilateral aid; cultural exchanges strengthened.
  • Aid Leadership: $2,150 Cr; hydropower prioritized over China.
  • Doklam Patrols: Intensified; 25 border talks stalled.
  • Trade Growth: $1.2B; India’s flood relief role prominent.
  • King Tobgay Visits: 2024-2025 India trips for hydropower funding.
  • Sakteng Resolution: Bhutan rejected China’s claims with India’s backing.
Myanmar
  • Rohingya Crisis: 1M+ refugees, 150K new arrivals in 2025; radicalization risks in camps.
  • Border Insurgency: 40K Rohingya in India; Arakan Army (AA) control in Rakhine.
  • Kaladan Delays: Multimodal project stalled due to coup; environmental concerns.
  • Drug Trafficking: $1B+ annually via Golden Triangle; impacts India’s northeast.
  • Post-2021 Coup Instability: Junta vs. resistance; refugee flows increase.
  • Security Threats: Insurgent groups using border for anti-India attacks.
  • BRI Ports: Kyaukpyu port; String of Pearls via Coco Islands.
  • Junta Aid: Chinese military support; anti-India junta alignment.
  • Rohingya Militants: Indirect backing for ARSA to destabilize borders.
  • Chinese Vessels: Research ships in Sittwe; maritime encirclement.
  • AA Anti-India: Arakan Army’s border incursions grow.
  • Act East Policy: BIMSTEC engagement; Asian Trilateral Highway.
  • Kaladan Multimodal: $484M project for connectivity.
  • Aid and Relief: $350 Cr; cyclone/earthquake support via Operation Brahma.
  • Rohingya Support: Hosting 40K; ASEAN 5-point consensus backed.
  • De-Hyphenation: Bilateral aid bypassing China’s influence.
  • Kaladan Progress: Advances despite AA control; $2B trade maintained.
  • Rohingya Camps: Strained; deportation stalled due to coup.
  • Earthquake Aid: 2025 Operation Brahma; junta-India arms deals.
  • China BRI: Port advances; India counters with highway projects.
  • Aid Surge: $350 Cr; ASEAN mediation role strengthened.
Sri Lanka
  • Fishermen Disputes: Katchatheevu island incidents; 2025 naval clashes reported.
  • Tamil Rights: 13th Amendment implementation delays; political autonomy stalled.
  • Debt Crisis: $51B total debt; 2022 default; IMF $2.9B bailout ongoing.
  • Chinese Influence: Port leases; radicalization risks in northern regions.
  • Trade Imbalances: $5B bilateral trade; tourism dependency ($2B from Indian visitors).
  • Political Instability: Post-2022 protests; Tamil devolution remains contentious.
  • Debt-Trap: Hambantota 99-year lease; $8B BRI loans.
  • String of Pearls: Colombo port expansion with Chinese funding.
  • $3.7B Oil Refinery: Chinese project in Hambantota strengthens influence.
  • Anti-India Nationalism: Rajapaksa revival fuels anti-India media.
  • Chinese Military: Drones, ships in Indian Ocean near Sri Lanka.
  • Neighborhood First: $4B aid (2022-2025); debt restructuring support.
  • CEPA Revival: Trade pact to boost $5B trade volume.
  • Adani Wind Project: $442M investment in Mannar for renewable energy.
  • Tamil Housing: 50,000 homes funded for post-war rehabilitation.
  • BrahMos Offer: Missile sales to enhance defense capabilities.
  • Aid Continuation: $300 Cr in 2025; supports crisis recovery under IMF.
  • Adani Operational: Wind project starts; $5B trade maintained.
  • Dissanayake-Modi Meet: 2025 talks on Tamil devolution progress.
  • Fishermen Talks: Ongoing; China’s restructuring terms accepted.
  • Radicalization Counter: Joint training with India to curb extremism.
Maldives
  • "India Out" Campaign: Muizzu’s 2023 election promise; Indian military withdrawal completed in 2024.
  • Debt Concerns: $440M reserves; 50% GDP debt owed to China.
  • Chinese Tilt: Anti-India nationalism; tourism dependency ($2B from Indian visitors).
  • Radicalization: Islamist risks; foreign influence in remote islands.
  • Maritime Security: Exclusive Economic Zone disputes; Chinese research vessels active.
  • Political Shifts: Muizzu’s pro-China stance; 2025 elections approach.
  • BRI Investments: $1.4B; Feydhoo Finolhu port expansion strengthens China’s presence.
  • "India Out" Nationalism: Campaign to reduce India’s influence; anti-India media amplified.
  • Chinese Vessels: Research ships in EEZ; maritime encirclement efforts.
  • Foreign Tourists: 20% rise in Chinese tourists; economic leverage against India.
  • Debt-Trap: Potential port leases; growing Chinese influence in parliament.
  • Neighborhood First: Greater Male Connectivity ($500M); $600 Cr aid package.
  • UPI/RuPay Launch: Digital payments to boost tourism transactions.
  • $565M LOC: Line of credit for infrastructure development.
  • IORA Chair: 2025 leadership to promote maritime cooperation.
  • Defense Cooperation: Joint exercises resumed; hydrographic surveys supported.
  • Modi Visit: July 2025; 8 agreements signed, including UPI rollout.
  • Muizzu India Visit: Defense ties restored; $500M trade sustained.
  • IORA Chair: Assumed; focus on climate resilience projects.
  • China FTA: Activated; India counters with housing aid.
  • Radicalization Counter: Joint intelligence sharing with India.
- India's Bilateral Relations with Non-Neighboring Countries -
Country Defense Cooperation Science & Technology Cooperation Economic Cooperation Aid & Development Assistance Key Developments (2025)
United States
  • INDUS-X Initiative: Expanded in 2025 with $500M for joint defense innovation in AI, quantum computing, and drones; 20+ startups co-developing counter-drone systems.
  • RELOS Agreement: Reciprocal Logistics Exchange (2023) enables mutual basing; 2025 Andaman Sea exercises with 10,000 troops.
  • MLSA: Mutual Logistics Support Agreement (2020), extended 2025, supports US carrier visits to Indian ports.
  • White Shipping Agreement: Maritime awareness (2016), upgraded 2025 with real-time satellite data for IOR security.
  • Navy-to-Navy MoU: Malabar 2025 exercise with QUAD; GE F-414 engine co-production for Tejas Mk2.
  • QUAD: Indo-Pacific security forum; 2025 Delhi summit focused on cyber defense and maritime patrols.
  • IPMDA: Indo-Pacific Maritime Domain Awareness (2022), 2025 rollout with $100M for Pacific surveillance.
  • $4B Drone Deal: MQ-9B Reaper acquisition (2024), deliveries started 2025; joint production in Hyderabad.
  • GE Jet Engine Co-Production: 80% tech transfer for F-414 engines, operational by late 2025.
  • COMCASA/BECA: Secure communications and geospatial agreements (2018/2020), 2025 upgrades for hypersonic tech sharing.
  • ASIA Alliance: Autonomous Systems Industry Alliance (2025), co-developing AI counter-UAS with Anduril-Mahindra.
  • COMPACT 21st Century: New 10-year defense framework (2025), $24B in defense sales since 2008.
  • IUSSTF: Indo-US Science & Technology Forum (2000), 2025 funding doubled to $50M for AI, quantum research, and biotech.
  • iCET: Critical and Emerging Technology Initiative (2022), 2025 focus on semiconductors, 6G; NISAR satellite launched April 2025.
  • Space Collaboration: NASA-ISRO NISAR Earth observation satellite; Artemis Accords signatory, planning joint lunar missions.
  • AI & Semiconductors: $10B chip fab in Gujarat operational in 2025; joint AI ethics framework signed.
  • TRUST Initiative: Transforming Relationship Utilizing Strategic Technology (2025), $1B for clean energy R&D.
  • Space Accelerator: Bilateral institute for lunar exploration, geospatial data sharing launched in 2025.
  • Cybersecurity: Joint R&D for quantum-resistant encryption, $200M fund in 2025.
  • TPF: US-India Trade Policy Forum (2010), 2025 meetings advanced Bilateral Trade Agreement (BTA) targeting $500B by 2030.
  • IPEF: Indo-Pacific Economic Framework (2022), India joined supply chains, clean energy, fair economy pillars in 2025.
  • BTA Negotiations: Finalized fall 2025, resolving tariffs and IPR disputes; $120B bilateral trade in 2024, $76B Indian exports.
  • Critical Minerals MoU: Signed 2025 for graphite, gallium supply chains with Commerce and Mines ministries.
  • COMPACT: Catalyzing Opportunities for Military Partnership, Accelerated Commerce & Technology (2025), $10B FDI target.
  • Investment Surge: $20B US FDI in 2025, focused on renewables, semiconductors.
  • Humanitarian Aid: $100M for disaster relief; supplied COVID vaccines to 40+ US states during 2021-22.
  • Development Financing: $1B via USAID for education, health; 10,000 Indian student grants annually.
  • Climate Cooperation: $500M for clean energy projects in Africa via I2U2 framework.
  • Health Initiatives: $50M for HIV/AIDS research; joint COVID vaccine trials in 2025.
  • Education Programs: $200M for STEM exchanges, 5,000 US students in India (2025).
  • Trump-Modi Meeting: February 2025, launched Indian Ocean Strategic Venture; $1B clean energy financing agreed.
  • 2+2 Intersessional: August 2025, advanced IMEC, I2U2; new IOR plurilateral partnerships.
  • Tariff Tensions: US imposed 25% tariffs (July 2025), raised to 50% (August); BTA talks ongoing to resolve.
  • Defense Sales: $24B since 2008; F-35 potential sale discussed in 2025.
  • NISAR Launch: Earth observation satellite operational (April 2025).
  • COMPACT Framework: 10-year defense partnership signed (2025).
  • TRUST Initiative: Launched for minerals, pharma supply chains (2025).
Russia
  • IRIGC-MTC: Inter-Governmental Commission on Military Technical Cooperation; 2025 meeting expedited S-400 deliveries.
  • S-400 Deal: $5.4B air defense system; full squadron operational in 2025.
  • AK-203 Production: 670,000 rifles joint venture; 100,000 units delivered to Indian Army in 2025.
  • BrahMos Missiles: Co-developed supersonic cruise missile; $1B exports to Philippines, Indonesia in 2025.
  • T-90 Tanks: Licensed production; 1,000 HP engine upgrade deal signed March 2025.
  • Su-30 MKI: Co-production; 2025 upgrade to Super Sukhoi with BrahMos integration.
  • Voronezh-DM Radar: $500M acquisition negotiated 2025 for missile early warning.
  • INDRA 2025: Naval and air exercises in Kaliningrad; 5,000 troops participated.
  • Ka-226T Helicopters: $1B deal for 200 units; production started in India (2025).
  • IRIGC-TEC: Trade, Economic, Scientific Cooperation Commission; 2025 focus on AI, quantum technology, and Arctic research.
  • Yamal LNG Stake: ONGC’s 20% share; 2025 expansion to Arctic LNG projects.
  • Rosatom Nuclear: Kudankulam Units 3-6 commissioned in 2025; $30B reactors deal for Units 7-8.
  • Space Cooperation: Gaganyaan astronaut training; 2025 joint Arctic monitoring satellite launched.
  • AI Research: $100M joint lab in Bengaluru for defense and energy applications (2025).
  • Trade Target: $100B by 2030; 2025 reached $68.7B, up from $12B pre-2022.
  • Energy Imports: 35% of India’s oil imports from Russia; 80% rupee-rouble settlement in 2025.
  • INSTC Operational: Full route operational in 2025, boosting trade by $20B.
  • EAEU FTA: Negotiations advanced in 2025; tariff cuts on pharma, machinery.
  • Diamond Trade: $2B rough diamond imports; 2025 MoU for direct sourcing.
  • Energy Aid: $100M in fertilizers and wheat to ease global food prices (2025).
  • Humanitarian Aid: $50M for Ukraine refugees hosted in India; medical supplies provided.
  • Arctic Development: $200M for sustainable energy projects via Rosatom (2025).
  • Doval-Putin Meet: August 2025; Putin visit planned for December 2025.
  • Defense Deliveries: S-400 fully operational; AK-203 production ramped up (2025).
  • Trade Boom: $68.7B in FY24-25; rupee-rouble mechanism at 80% adoption.
  • INSTC Launch: Chennai-Vladivostok corridor tested; $20B trade boost.
  • US Tariffs Impact: 50% tariffs on Russian oil buyers; India-Russia trade unaffected.
  • BRICS/SCO: Anti-terror resolution passed; NDB approved $10B loans (2025).
Saudi Arabia
  • Al-Mohed Al-Hindi: 2025 naval exercise with 5,000 troops; focus on counter-piracy in Arabian Sea.
  • Defense MoU: Signed 2025 for missile technology transfer; $500M arms deal talks.
  • IMEC Security: Joint patrols for India-Middle East-Europe Corridor; 2025 maritime agreement.
  • Cyber Defense: 2025 collaboration for threat intelligence sharing.
  • Space Cooperation: 2025 MoU for satellite technology; joint climate monitoring project.
  • AI Partnership: $200M fund for sand technology, desalination research in 2025.
  • Renewable Tech: Joint R&D for solar panel efficiency; $100M investment.
  • IMEC MoU: 2025 progress on rail/port integration; $20B Saudi pledge.
  • $100B Investment: Pledged for green hydrogen, refineries, and infrastructure (2025).
  • Strategic Partnership Council: 2025 meeting; $52.75B trade (2022-23 base), targeting $100B by 2030.
  • Energy Trade: 5% of India’s oil imports; $30B crude imports in 2025.
  • Food Security: $1B investment in Indian agriculture via SPC (2025).
  • PGII Pledge: $20B for infrastructure in South Asia (2025).
  • Climate Fund: $1B for renewable energy projects in Africa, South Asia.
  • Humanitarian Aid: $50M for Yemen relief via India (2025).
  • Modi Visit: April 2025; 2nd SPC meeting, finalized $20B IMEC investment.
  • Trade Expansion: $52.75B in 2022-23; 2025 growth to $60B.
  • Energy MoU: Green hydrogen, renewables cooperation signed (2025).
  • Investment Office: Saudi Ministry of Investment office opened in Delhi (2025).
  • IMEC Progress: Rail feasibility studies completed; port integration started.
Israel
  • i4F: India-Israel Industrial R&D Fund (2021), $40M for 2025; 11 projects in cyber, AI, and drones.
  • Barak-8 Missiles: $2B deal (2018); 2025 naval variant upgrades completed.
  • Drone Technology: Heron TP drones; 2025 co-production of Hermes 900 in Bengaluru.
  • Staff Talks: 13th round in 2025; focus on joint missile production.
  • I2U2 Defense: 2025 exercises with UAE, US; counter-drone systems developed.
  • Tejas Interest: Egypt eyes Tejas via Israel tech integration; 2025 talks.
  • Cyber Defense: 2025 MoU for joint cybersecurity platforms.
  • Space Cooperation: 2025 joint lunar probe; ISRO geospatial data sharing.
  • AI & Cyber: $100M fund for 2025; joint labs in Bengaluru for cybersecurity.
  • Agriculture Tech: Drip irrigation R&D; $50M for smart farming in 2025.
  • Quantum Computing: 2025 MoU for joint research; $20M fund.
  • I2U2: 2025 summit in UAE; $2B for food parks, clean energy in Gujarat.
  • Abraham Accords Benefits: $2B trade in 2025; tech startups co-funded.
  • BIA: Bilateral Investment Agreement (2025), first with an OECD country.
  • Trade Growth: $2B bilateral trade; $10B target by 2030.
  • Fintech: $200M investment in Indian startups (2025).
  • Gaza Relief: $200M via UN for humanitarian aid (2025).
  • African Development: $50M for Egyptian agriculture via I2U2 (2025).
  • Water Tech: $20M for desalination projects in Africa (2025).
  • Strategic Partnership: Elevated to comprehensive level in 2025; Nile Medal awarded to Modi.
  • Defense Meeting: 2025 joint committee; Tejas deal with Egypt advanced.
  • Trade Surge: $2B bilateral with Israel; $7.26B with Egypt (2021-22 base).
  • I2U2 Summit: 2025 in UAE; $100M food security projects launched.
  • Space MoU: Signed 2025 for joint satellite; NISAR progress reported.
Iran
  • INSTC Security: 2025 joint patrols for International North-South Transport Corridor; $100M naval aid.
  • Chabahar Defense: 2025 MoU for missile technology; counter-terrorism training programs.
  • Naval Exercises: 2025 IONS joint drill in Persian Gulf with 2,000 troops.
  • INSTC Technology: 2025 digital corridor; satellite data for logistics optimization.
  • Oil Tech: $50M for refinery upgrades; joint R&D in 2025.

- All important agreements & treaties -
Name Year Parties Purpose Special Notes
Treaties
Indus Water Treaty 1960 India, Pakistan To allocate Indus River waters, with India controlling eastern rivers and Pakistan western rivers, to prevent water conflicts. World Bank-brokered; India gets 30% water; ratified 1960; 2025 suspension by India post-Pahalgam attack; arbitration at PCA; $100M aid withheld.
Lahore Declaration 1999 India, Pakistan To reduce nuclear risks and promote peace through bilateral dispute resolution and confidence-building measures. Signed February 21, 1999; includes hotlines; undermined by Kargil War; 2025 ceasefire fragile; binding under international law.
Nepal Treaty of Peace and Friendship 1950 India, Nepal To promote peace, friendship, open borders, trade, and mutual security assistance. Signed July 31, 1950; ratified; 2025 review amid border disputes; $700M Indian aid; $1.5B hydropower trade.
Panchsheel Agreement 1954 India, China To establish five principles of peaceful coexistence for trade and Tibet relations. Signed April 29, 1954; ratified; NAM foundation; 2025 revival talks amid border tensions; $135B trade, $100B deficit.
Shimla Agreement 1972 India, Pakistan To establish bilateral dispute resolution, respect Line of Control, and normalize post-1971 war relations. Signed July 2, 1972; ratified; 2025 suspension post-Pahalgam; ceasefire reaffirmed; $100M aid withheld.
UN Convention on the Law of the Sea (UNCLOS) 1982 169 states, EU To define legal framework for ocean use, including territorial seas, EEZs, and resource rights. India ratified 1995; 170 parties; 2025 ISA mining rules; $100B deep-sea resources; India’s EEZ 2.37M sq km.
Paris Agreement 2015 196 parties To limit global warming to below 2°C, ideally 1.5°C, via nationally determined contributions. India ratified 2016; 2025 NDC update targets 45% emissions reduction; $1T climate finance; 500 GW renewables by 2030.
Nuclear Non-Proliferation Treaty (NPT) 1968 191 parties To prevent nuclear weapons spread, promote disarmament, and enable peaceful nuclear energy. India not party; NSG waiver 2008; 2025 NSG membership push; $10B nuclear trade.
Comprehensive Test Ban Treaty (CTBT) 1996 187 signatories To ban all nuclear explosions for military or civilian purposes. India signed 1996, not ratified; 2025 entry-into-force push; supports IAEA; no tests since 1998.
Ramsar Convention 1971 171 parties To conserve wetlands of international importance. India ratified 1982; 82 sites (e.g., Sundarbans); 2025: 4 new sites; $50M conservation funding.
Montreal Protocol 1987 198 parties To phase out ozone-depleting substances. India ratified 1992; Kigali Amendment (2016); 2025 compliance 100%; $300M MLF funding.
Basel Convention 1989 191 parties To control transboundary hazardous waste movements. India ratified 1992; 2025 e-waste rules strengthened; $100M technical aid.
Stockholm Convention 2001 186 parties To protect human health from persistent organic pollutants (POPs). India ratified 2006; 2025 POPs list update; $200M for elimination projects.
ICCPR 1966 173 parties To protect civil and political rights, including freedom of speech and fair trials. India ratified 1979; 2025 human rights review; NHRC strengthened; $50M for implementation.
ICESCR 1966 171 parties To promote economic, social, and cultural rights like health and education. India ratified 1979; 2025 SDG report; $300M for poverty alleviation.
CEDAW 1979 189 parties To eliminate discrimination against women in all spheres. India ratified 1993; 2025 review; Women’s Reservation Bill implemented; $100M for gender programs.
CAT 1984 173 parties To prevent torture and cruel treatment. India signed 1997, not ratified; 2025 domestic bill passed; NHRC monitoring; $50M for training.
CRC 1989 196 parties To protect children’s rights to survival, development, and participation. India ratified 1992; 2025 child labor laws strengthened; $200M for education.
CRPD 2006 182 parties To protect rights of persons with disabilities, including accessibility. India ratified 2007; 2025 accessibility laws; $100M for inclusive education.
Ganga Water Sharing Treaty 1996 India, Bangladesh To equitably share Ganga waters during dry season (January-May). Signed December 12, 1996; 30-year term; 2025 renewal talks; 35,000 cusecs for India, 34,500 for Bangladesh.
India-Bhutan Treaty of Friendship 2007 India, Bhutan To promote peace, cooperation, and mutual security, including Indian military training in Bhutan. Revised from 1949; 2025 review; $2,150M aid; $10B hydropower ventures.
India-US Civil Nuclear Agreement 2008 India, US To enable civil nuclear cooperation with IAEA safeguards and NSG waiver. Signed 2008; 2025 Jaitapur reactors extension; $10B trade; 6,000 MW capacity planned.
Korea-India CEPA 2009 India, South Korea To liberalize trade in goods, services, and investment, reducing tariffs on 90% of goods. Effective 2010; $25.35B trade; 2025 digital trade review; $10B Gujarat chip fab.
India-Japan CEPA 2011 India, Japan To boost trade and investment by reducing tariffs. Effective 2011; $22B trade; 2025 services upgrade; $15B MAHSR investment.
India-Mauritius CEPA 2021 India, Mauritius To eliminate tariffs on 90% of goods, promoting textiles, fish, and services trade. Effective April 2021; $1B trade; 2025 digital economy review.
India-UAE CEPA 2022 India, UAE To reduce tariffs on 90% of goods, targeting $100B trade by 2030. Effective May 2022; $100.5B trade; 2025 review; 80% tariff cuts; $75B investment.
India-Australia ECTA 2022 India, Australia To eliminate tariffs on 85% Australian goods, rising to 90% by 2026. Effective December 29, 2022; $25B trade; 2025 BTA talks; $10B minerals investment.
India-UAE BIT 2024 India, UAE To protect investments against unfair treatment with dispute resolution. Signed February 13, 2024; $75B FDI potential; 2025 implementation; oil, tech focus.
India-Oman CEPA 2025 India, Oman To liberalize trade in goods, services, and investment, targeting $10B trade. Negotiated 2025; 90% tariff elimination; $5B port, energy investment.
India-UK FTA Ongoing (2025) India, UK To reduce tariffs and barriers for $100B trade by 2030. 14 rounds by 2025; $40B trade; 2025 investment chapter finalized; services focus.
India-EFTA BTA 2024 India, EFTA (Iceland, Liechtenstein, Norway, Switzerland) To promote trade and investment with $100B investment commitment. Signed March 10, 2024; $100B FDI pledge; 90% tariff cuts; $1B trade boost.
India-US BTA Ongoing (2025) India, US To address tariffs, IPR, and barriers for $500B trade by 2030. Talks started 2025; $131.84B trade; 2025 tariff resolution partial for $18B goods.
India-EU BTA Ongoing (2025) India, EU To liberalize trade in goods, services, and investment. 16 rounds; 2025 push for $100B trade; investment chapter finalized.
Abraham Accords 2020 Israel, UAE, Bahrain, Sudan, Morocco To normalize Israel-Arab relations, promoting trade and cooperation. India not party; US-brokered; $10B UAE-Israel trade; 2025 I2U2 expansion; $50B investment; Gaza conflict strains.
Joint Comprehensive Plan of Action (JCPOA) 2015 Iran, P5+1 (US, UK, France, Germany, Russia, China, EU) To limit Iran’s nuclear program for sanctions relief, preventing nuclear weapon development. India not party; implemented 2016; US withdrew 2018; 2025 talks stalled; $100B sanctions relief; India supports energy trade.
Trans-Pacific Partnership (TPP) 2016 12 countries (Australia, Brunei, Canada, Chile, Japan, Malaysia, Mexico, New Zealand, Peru, Singapore, Vietnam, US) To eliminate tariffs and set high-standard trade rules in Asia-Pacific. India not party; US withdrew 2017; evolved into CPTPP; $10T economy; India observer interest.
Comprehensive and Progressive Agreement for Trans-Pacific Partnership (CPTPP) 2018 11 countries (Australia, Brunei, Canada, Chile, Japan, Malaysia, Mexico, New Zealand, Peru, Singapore, Vietnam; UK joined 2023) To liberalize trade with high standards in labor, environment, and IP. India not party; 13.5% global GDP; 90% tariffs eliminated; 2025 India accession interest.
Agreements
Digital Economy Partnership Agreement (DEPA) 2020 Chile, New Zealand, Singapore To facilitate digital trade and create a digital economy framework. India observer interest; aspirants include China, Canada; promotes open internet; 2025 expansion talks.
Ashgabat Agreement 2011 Iran, Oman, Turkmenistan, Uzbekistan, India, Kazakhstan, Pakistan To develop multimodal transport corridor between Central Asia and Persian Gulf. India joined 2018; 2025 Chabahar-Zahedan rail completed; synchronizes with INSTC.
BBIN Motor Vehicles Agreement (BBIN MVA) 2015 Bangladesh, Bhutan, India, Nepal To enable seamless vehicle movement across borders, boosting regional trade. Signed June 15, 2015; Bhutan opted out temporarily; 2025 trial runs expanded; 30% trade cost reduction.
Coordinated Border Management Plan (CBMP) 2016 India, Bangladesh To reduce cross-border crimes through coordinated patrols and intelligence sharing. Post-2015 LBA; 50% cattle smuggling reduction; 2025 narcotics focus; 4,096 km border patrols.
Double Taxation Avoidance Agreement (DTAA) Various (e.g., India-US 1989) India with 88 countries To avoid double taxation, allocate taxing rights, and promote trade. 86 in force; TDS 10-15%; 2025 rupee settlements; $200B FDI facilitated.
India-Bangladesh Land Boundary Agreement (LBA) 2015 India, Bangladesh To resolve border enclaves, exchanging 111 Indian for 51 Bangladeshi enclaves. Implemented 2015 via 100th Amendment; 162 enclaves exchanged; 51,000 resettled; 2025 border stability.
Interim Agreement on Sharing of Waters of Six Rivers 2022 India, Bangladesh To facilitate interim water sharing of six rivers, promoting equitable distribution. Agreed 2022; JRC data exchange; 2025 technical talks for Teesta precursor.
Kushiyara Agreement 2022 India, Bangladesh To share Kushiyara River waters, benefiting Assam and Sylhet. Signed September 6, 2022; 153 cusecs for Bangladesh; Rahimpur Canal enabled; flood risk reduction.
Migration and Mobility Partnership Arrangement 2023 India, Australia To promote mobility for students, researchers, and professionals. Signed May 24, 2023; MATES pilot (3,000 places/year); 2025 expansion to 5,000 places; $200M tech, renewables.
Mutual Logistics Support Agreement (MLSA) 2020 India, US To provide mutual logistical support for military operations and exercises. Extended 2025; $24B defense sales; 2025 Andaman exercises; IOR focus.
Reciprocal Exchange of Logistics Agreement (RELOS) 2021 India, US To enable reciprocal access to military logistics facilities. Extends LEMOA; 2025 Andaman Sea exercises with 10,000 troops; QUAD support.
Reciprocal Provision of Supplies and Services Agreement (RPSS) 2020 India, US To allow reciprocal supply of materials and services during operations. Part of COMPACT (2025); $24B sales; 2025 hypersonic tech sharing.
SAFTA (South Asian Free Trade Area) 2006 SAARC countries To promote intraregional trade by reducing tariffs. Effective 2006; $30B trade; 2025 digital trade review; BIMSTEC alternative.
Teesta Water Agreement Ongoing (1983 ad-hoc) India, Bangladesh To equitably share Teesta River waters for irrigation. Ad-hoc 1983 (India 39%, Bangladesh 36%); 2011 draft stalled; 2025 JRC talks; China $1B counter-offer.
White Shipping Agreement 2016 India, US To exchange merchant vessel movement information for maritime security. Upgraded 2025 with satellite data; 2025 IMEC integration; IOR piracy focus.

Initiatives
China-Africa Cooperation Vision 2035 2021 China, 55 African countries, African Union Observer/Partner To align China’s 2035 Vision with AU’s Agenda 2063 for sustainable development, trade, and investment in Africa. 9 Programs (e.g., Medical & Health, Trade Promotion, Green Development); FOCAC Ministerial Conferences. 3-Year Action Plan (2025-2027) launched; $60B investment pledged; 500+ agricultural experts dispatched. $300B trade target by 2035; 140+ projects in infrastructure; supports AU's PIDA and AMV.
Chinese Debt Diplomacy Ongoing (BRI 2013) China with 150+ countries (e.g., Sri Lanka, Pakistan, Laos) Critic/Alternative Provider Strategic lending for infrastructure to gain influence, often leading to debt restructuring for asset control. BRI Corridors; Debt-for-Equity Swaps; Policy Banks (e.g., CDB, Exim Bank). Zambia Debt Restructuring (June 2025); $35B repayments from poorest countries; $22B from 75 vulnerable nations. $1T+ BRI loans; $380B global debt; critiques as "debt trap"; India counters with PGII.
Dakar Action Plan 2021 China, 53 African countries, AU Partner via AU To implement FOCAC outcomes for 2022-2024, focusing on health, trade, and green development. 9 Programs (e.g., Poverty Reduction, Digital Innovation); FOCAC Secretariat. Beijing Action Plan (2025-2027) extended; $11B+ in climate funding; 1B COVID vaccines delivered. $300B trade; 24 ag-tech centers; supports AU's Agenda 2063.
Empowered Arctic Policy Group 2022 India (inter-ministerial) Lead To implement India’s Arctic Policy for sustainable development and climate research. Working Groups (Science, Environment, Connectivity); MoES Oversight. Winter Expedition (March 2025); Himadri Station upgrades; $50M funding. 140 GW renewable potential; EEZ mining rights; Ny-Ålesund research.
Freedom of Navigation Operations (FONOP) Ongoing (1979) US-led (allies: Japan, Australia, India) Participant To challenge excessive maritime claims, ensuring freedom of navigation under UNCLOS. US DoD FON Program; QUAD Maritime Initiative; Regional Fusion Centers. USS Carl Vinson FONOP (Jan 21, 2025); 7 operations in South China Sea; $600B defense commitment. Challenges China's 9-dash line; $1.6T trade secured; 97% global trade protected.

Mechanisms
Confidence Building Measures (CBMs) Ongoing (1975 CSCE) Global (UN, OSCE, India-Pakistan) Participant To build trust via transparency, communication, and risk reduction in security domains. Hotlines; Joint Exercises; OSCE Vienna Document; UN Register of Conventional Arms. India-Pakistan DGMO Talks (May 2025); LoC ceasefire extended; cyber CBMs. Reduces escalation; 50% cattle smuggling drop; $100M nuclear CBMs.
Regional Anti-Terrorist Structure (RATS) 2004 SCO members (China, India, Russia, etc.) Member To coordinate counter-terrorism, anti-extremism efforts in Eurasia. Council; Executive Committee; Joint Exercises; Database of Terrorists. 44th Council Meeting (June 2025, Cholpon-Ata); Pahalgam attack condemned; Cobalt-2025 exercise. 300+ terrorists arrested; $20B trade security; RATS database 1,000+ entries.
SWIFT Mechanism 1973 11,500+ institutions in 200 countries Member To facilitate secure cross-border payments and financial messaging. MT Messages; ISO 20022; Governance Committee; Regional Groups. Blockchain Integration (Nov 2025); live trials for CBDCs; $150T annual payments. $150T annual transactions; 98% global finance; India’s $1.2T remittances.
WTO Dispute Settlement System (DSS) 1995 164 WTO members Member To resolve trade disputes via panels and Appellate Body. Panels; Appellate Body; DSB; Compliance Proceedings. 32 Appeals into Void (April 2025); MC14 reform talks; 400+ cases since 1995. 95% settlement rate; $1.2T trade secured; India's 200+ wins.
WTO Geneva Package 2022 WTO members Member To address MC12 outcomes on fisheries, e-commerce, and emergency responses. Fisheries Subsidies Agreement; COVID Waiver; Food Security Declaration. Implementation Review (2025); $100B vaccine waiver extended; fisheries $22B trade. $1.3T trade; 10% global fisheries; counters subsidies $35B/year.
WTO Trade Facilitation Agreement (Bali 2013) 2013 164 WTO members Member To simplify customs procedures for faster trade. Committees (Implementation, Technical Assistance); Transparency Portal. Full Implementation (2025); $1T trade boost; 92.9% ASEAN rate. $1T trade cost savings; 14.5% cut for LDCs; India's $400B exports.
WTO TRIPS Agreement 1995 164 WTO members Member To set minimum IP standards for trade-related aspects. Council for TRIPS; Review Mechanism; Enforcement Provisions. Implementation Review (2025); 88/88 notifications; COVID waiver extended. $5B IP trade; $200B FDI; India's $50M pharma exports.
WTO Agreement on Agriculture (AoA) 1994 164 WTO members Member To reform agricultural trade via market access, domestic support, export subsidies. Committee on Agriculture; Special Safeguards; Domestic Support Notifications. MC14 Talks (2025); $380B subsidies; India's MSP defense. $1.4T ag trade; $380B subsidies; India's $400B exports; LDC flexibilities.

Training Course (April 2025); 28 officials; e-commerce moratorium. $6T services trade; 70% global GDP; India's $300B exports.
WTO Doha Round 2001 164 WTO members Member To lower trade barriers and reform rules for development. Trade Negotiations Committee; Working Groups (Agriculture, Services); Aid for Trade. MC14 Renewal (2025); $1.3T trade potential; stalled since 2015. $1T Doha gains; 20 areas negotiated; India's $100B ag priority.
WTO Special and Differential Treatment (S&DT) 1979 (Enabling Clause) WTO members (developing/LDCs) Beneficiary To provide flexibilities for developing countries in trade rules. 88 Provisions; CTD; S&D Monitoring Mechanism. China Foregoes (Sep 2025); G-90 Proposals; 88/88 notifications. $1T LDC trade; 10-year transitions; India's MSP protection.
WTO Special Safeguard Mechanism (SSM) Ongoing (Nairobi 2015) WTO developing members Advocate To allow temporary tariff increases on ag imports for surges/prices. Negotiation Modalities; G-33 Proposals; Agriculture Committee. Fast-Track Negotiations (2025); MC14 priority; $11B ag protection. 10% tariff cap; protects LDCs; India's $400B exports safeguarded.
WTO Information Technology Agreement (ITA 1 & 2) 1996 (ITA1); 2015 (ITA2) 84 participants (97% IT trade) Member (ITA1) To eliminate tariffs on IT products for global trade growth. ITA Committee; Product Coverage Review; NTM Work Program. Thematic Sessions (2025-2026); AI links; $1.6T trade covered. $1.6T IT trade; 201 products tariff-free; India's $200B exports.

India-Israel Industrial R&D and Innovation Fund (i4F) Bilateral R&D Fund 2021 India, Israel To promote, facilitate, and support joint industrial R&D projects between Indian and Israeli companies, leading to co-development and commercialization of innovative technologies benefiting both countries. Supports India's "Make in India" and Israel's innovation ecosystem; $2B potential trade; strengthens tech ties; 75% Indian funding for startups; 70% project success rate; $100M IPR development.

Managed by EverSource Capital (Everstone Group and Lightsource BP JV); investments in mid-to-large green projects; equity and debt financing; climate-resilient infrastructure. $500 million (initial £240 million anchor from NIIF and DFID, raised to $500 million) First investment in Ayana Renewable Power (2025); $70 million from bp; $1 billion mobilized for renewables; 2025 focus on $200 million for EV and solar; $50 million for water projects. $1 billion in green projects; 5 GW renewable capacity; 10,000 jobs; counters BRI; India's $1T climate finance; $200M annual returns; 30% IRR for investors; $100M carbon credits.

- Current affairs -
- Chapter 1 - NEIGHBOURHOOD - 1. CRISIS IN NEIGHBOURHOOD - India’s neighboring countries are witnessing political and economic turmoil. These include Sri Lanka, Pakistan, Afghanistan, Nepal, and Myanmar. Such political and economic upheavals pose a challenge for India to balance its ties with these countries, since it has been accused of acting as big brother and of interference in internal affairs of its neighborhood. However, it has been argued that India has learnt its lessons from the past and has displayed a careful approach towards the neighboring crises. In this context, let us understand the lessons learnt by India and what more needs to be done. - STRONG ARM POLICY APPROACH - India has been accused of acting like Big-Brother by its smaller neighbours. India being the most powerful and resourceful country in the region likes to see South Asian region as its backward and is the regional hegemon in the region. - In recent past, India has been accused of following strong arm tactics under popularly known approach of muscular foreign policy. -
CountryActionContext
PakistanGovernment had put Pakistan on notice for terror attacks and cancelled Foreign Secretary level talks.Countering terrorism
Sri LankaMahinda Rajapaksa government, seen to be close to China, was voted out, with reports that Indian intelligence played a role in facilitating opposition talks.Countering Chinese influence
NepalPM K.P. Sharma Oli's coalition government had fallen apart, with a similar nudge reported from New Delhi.Countering Chinese influence
- These measures have primarily been to counter the Chinese influence in its neighbourhood. But such an approach did not have support our neighbours and was resented. - INCLUSIVE APPROACH TOWARDS NEIGHBOURHOOD - Myanmar (Military coup), Nepal, Afghanistan (Taliban replaced Ashraf Ghani led govt.), Pakistan (PM Imran Khan lost the confidence Vote), and Sri Lanka (Political change owing to economic crisis) all have witnessed change in the ruling regime. - In contrast to the earlier accusations of being a Big brother, has not been held responsible in any of its neighbouring countries for attempting to interfere in their political processes. Thus, India has displayed a more neutral approach. - India has abandoned “one size fits all” approach to the region. - In Myanmar, India has strengthened ties with Military Junta. - India has been supportive of change in ruling party in Nepal and Sri Lanka. - India has almost ignored the change of ruling party in Pakistan. - India now is focused more on people in neighbourhood rather than just those in power. - Diplomatic maneuvering with the Afghan and Pak diplomats, despite strained ties with both nations, to send 50,000 MT wheat to support Afghan population facing humanitarian crisis. - In Sri Lanka, India would always be guided by the best interests of the people of Sri Lanka expressed through democratic processes. - Toning down of rhetoric on domestic issues in the neighbourhood. - LESSONS INDIA NEEDS TO LEARN - Neutral position in neighbourhood is far more challenging than neutrality shown by India in case of Ukraine war or China’s moves on Hong Kong. This is because the fallout of crisis in neighbourhood has direct impact on India unlike the other cases. - In the neighbourhood, India needs to prepare itself for the need for aid and loans or an influx of refugees, as movements that develop in one neighbouring country are often mirrored in another. - Populist policies shown by the ruling parties in the neighbourhood have not worked. Examples being Sinhala Nationalism in Sri Lanka, Anti–India sentiment in Nepal. - Economy matters in bringing political stability. All neighbouring countries were under severe economic strain. - India must also assess the impact of economic and political vulnerabilities on smaller neighbouring countries that could be exploited by global powers as they seek a more direct influence in the region. - India must find newer ways to energise regional groupings such as Bay of Bengal Initiative for Multi-Sectoral Technical and Economic Cooperation (BIMSTEC) and Bangladesh, Bhutan, India, Nepal (BBIN) Initiative, and even to reconsider SAARC. - 2. INDIA'S NEIGHBOURHOOD POLICY - - CHALLENGES IN INDIA’S NEIGHBOURHOOD - India's neighbourhood is a complex one. Certain features of India’s neighbourhood are: - Baggage of colonialism: - Region has a shared past of colonial exploitation. - Large population: - South Asia is largest region in world by population. - Diversities: - South Asia is marked by large ethnic, religious and linguistic diversities. - Unsettled & Unscientific Boundaries of the region have not been formed in a very scientific way; this often creates tensions between countries. - Least economically and logistically integrated region: - South Asia is the least integrated regions with tremendous deficits in infrastructure, connectivity, and interdependence. - Dysfunctional Regional multilateralism: - Institutions like ASEAN and EU have failed to develop in South Asian context. SAARC remains a dysfunctional body. - Exposed to geopolitical competition: - South Asian is now being exposed to various geopolitical competition dynamics because of rise of China and increasing US influence in the region. - Low human development: - South Asia region is one of the poorer regions of the world, with low social development. - NEW DYNAMICS IN THE REGION - India has been accused of acting like ‘Big-Brother’ by its smaller neighbours in the region. India being the most powerful and resourceful country in the region likes to see the South Asian region as its backward and is the regional hegemon in the region. - An increasingly assertive China has been making strong outreach to countries in the region, China’s unsettled border issue India, long India-China standoff on India China border, increasing naval presence of China in Indian Ocean and the increasing power asymmetry between India and China in terms of economic and military has been creating security dilemma for India against China. - India and Pakistan have fought four wars since 1947. Both nations have nuclear weapons, Pakistan’s continued support for extremism and terrorism in Kashmir and claim over Kashmir has long created tensions between India and Pakistan. China-Pak axis has been concretising in the continental neighbourhood in terms of the CPEC corridor and the influence on Taliban in Afghanistan. - Taliban's takeover of Afghanistan and the US withdrawal poses threats to India's interest in the Central Asia besides posing the possibility of increase Pak-Taliban supported terrorism in Kashmir. - India has been aligning with the US in the form of Quad and Malabar naval exercise to counter the Chinese influence in the maritime arena. AUKUS security partnership is giving new direction to the security aspect of the Indo-Pacific concept. - India's structural dominance in the South Asia has been facing challenges because of rising Chinese influence. Neighbouring countries are using the China-Card in their negotiations with India. - EVENTS IN INDIA’S NEIGHBOURHOOD - Challenges to India to increasing influence of China: - Growing competition for influence in South Asia by carrying out various infrastructure projects and encircling India under the String of Pearls theory. Example - Sri Lanka, Myanmar etc. - China - Pakistan axis has considerable influence on the Taliban regime in Afghanistan. - Chinese aggression along the Line of Actual Control (LAC) poses the possibility of a Two Front War on India's norther borders. - China extended a $1.4-billion Line of Credit to Pakistan. A new defense pact has been signed between China and Pakistan. - All SAARC countries are part of Chinese BRI Project except India and Bhutan. - Most South Asian countries are under Chinese debt pressure. China has started to provide partial debt waivers to the Maldives and Sri Lanka. - Territorial claims of China in the region - Due to unsettled border between India & China, China claims parts of Ladakh and Arunachal as its territory. - Chin has laid claim to Bhutan’s Sakteng natural reserves. - China has annexed around 150 hectares of land along Nepal border. - India has witnessed border issues with Nepal along the Kalapani. Nepal has amended its constitution to include the disputed territory within its political map. - In Sri Lanka, domestic politics and Chinese influence is impacting its ties with India. - Regional SAARC has taken a backseat in India's regional pursuits - Format of SAARC is outdated and does not serve complex, fluid regional cooperation agenda any longer. India has refused to attend SAARC meeting in Pakistan over terrorism issue. - Pakistan is pursuing its regional connectivity goals exclusively with China (CPEC). While India does not align itself with Chinese BRI project. India started focussing on Indian Ocean region and towards eastern neighbours of India and BIMSTEC. This has led to a split of subcontinent between India and Pakistan which has effectively stagnated SAARC. - Recently a domestic political turmoil was witnessed in Pakistan (ouster and arrest of Imran Khan) which testifies the critical involvement of Army and the ISI (deep state of Pakistan) in not only domestic politics but also degerming the larger foreign policy objectives of Pakistan and ultimately its relationship with the neighbouring countries. - India has revived BIMSTEC and worked in BBIN (Bangladesh-Bhutan-India-Nepal) quadrilateral for a framework on motor vehicle & water governance. - However, India should understand that India's neighbouring countries other than Pakistan have shown interest in the working of the SAARC. - INDIA'S RESPONSE TO CHALLENGES IN NEIGHBOURHOOD - India has been pushing forward its Neighborhood first Policy and the SAGAR (Security and Growth for All in the Region) strategy to handle these recent challenges. - ‘Neighbourhood First’ policy - Under its 'Neighbourhood First' policy, Government is committed to developing friendly and mutually beneficial relations with all its neighbors. India is an active development partner and is involved in several projects in these countries. - India’s policy of ‘Neighbourhood First’ focuses on creating mutually beneficial, people-oriented, regional frameworks for stability and prosperity. - Our engagement with these countries is based on a consultative, non-reciprocal and outcome-oriented approach, which focuses on delivering benefits like greater connectivity, improved infrastructure, stronger development cooperation in various sectors area, security and broader people-to-people contacts. - With Pakistan, Government desires normal neighbourly relations and is committed to addressing all outstanding issues bilaterally and peacefully in accordance with the Shimla Agreement and the Lahore Declaration. However, any meaningful dialogue can only be held in an atmosphere free from terror, hostility and violence. The onus is on Pakistan to create such a conducive atmosphere. - Infrastructure - India has stepped up delivery of Infrastructure in the region. - Completion of railway lines to Bangladesh and Nepal, riverine projects, ferry service to the Maldives, identifying other services to Sri Lanka and IOR islands. - India has provided line of credit to the tune of $ 400 million to Maldives. - Welcoming entry of other powers to counter China - India has welcomed the U.S.’s new military dialogue with the Maldives. - America’s Millennium Challenge Corporation’s (MCC) projects in Afghanistan, Bhutan, Sri Lanka, Nepal and Bangladesh - The Millennium Challenge Corporation (MCC) is an innovative and independent U.S. foreign assistance agency that is helping lead the fight against global poverty. MCC forms partnerships with developing countries who are committed to good governance, economic freedom and investing in their citizens. - Diplomatic efforts - India has stepped up the collaboration in the Quadrilateral security Dialogue. - Malabar naval exercise has been expanded to include Australia. - WAY FORWARD - Analysis of India's foreign policy approach in neighbourhood - India was the first country to warn the global community about dangers of Belt and Road Initiativ (BRI). This framing of problems is widely accepted by most global powers. - India has also managed to shape the global discourse on the Indo-Pacific. - China's efforts of teaching lessons to India using force (Galwan valley) has failed. - India has been pursuing its strategic and economic strategies far more independently. It effectively resisted the Chinese aggression, developed infrastructure along the LAC, reduced trade dependence on China in strategic sectors and has galvanised global support in the Indo-Pacific in the form of QUAD. - Neighbourhood policy should not be viewed with a narrow lens of wins or losses. A region shaped by two major powers will never follow a linear trajectory. Also, the smaller countries in the region have become more assertive and have been pursuing their policies independently. - The traditional notion of India-Pak rivalry constraining India's foreign Policy options has given way to the increasing influence of China. - South Asia today is being viewed as pivotal to the concept of Indo-Pacific because of the India's efforts. It is because of this that the BIMSTEC has gained currency replacing the SAARC in the Indian foreign policy circles. - Focus of BIMSTEC also outlines India's focus on its eastern frontier aligning it with its Look East Policy. - India has been focusing on defence modernisation and cooperation which is visible in the recent signing of foundational defence agreements with US, S-400 missile deal and purchase of Rafael military aircrafts. - Future course of action - India's engagement with our neighbouring countries should not be episodic. It should not be event-oriented; it should be process-oriented. India needs to have a plan for continuous engagement at various levels. - India has realised that there is a need for greater connectivity and integration in the region, especially because of the increasing Chinese influence in its neighbourhood. - Further to check the growing Chinese influence in the neighbourhood, India should focus on creating interdependence in the region with the aim of extracting strategic leverage. - We know that India has taken over various connectivity initiatives, in terms of energy, interdependence, infrastructural connectivity, grants and loans. - However, it is being said that these connectivity initiatives will be meaningless if there is lack of economic integration and easy movement of people and capital. - India should not be averse to the idea of cooperation in the subcontinent by the way of SAARC. - Also, if India moves away from SAARC, it is quite possible that China can be invited to this forum. This will make things even more difficult for India. - 3. LOW EMPHASIS ON INTERNATIONAL LAW IN INDIA - India since its independence has played an active role in international affairs and maintained cordial relations with different countries. India has played decisive and important role at various international forum such as United Nations General Assembly, different multilateral forums (G-20, BRICS, QUAD, IBSA etc.). India has played an active role in shaping international law on terrorism by proposing a Comprehensive Convention on International Terrorism (CCIT) and initiated International Solar Alliance towards harnessing renewable energy. Despite many successes in the field of international relations, India has not utilized International Law effectively in its advantage. Thus, this article suggests that India’s ambition of punching above its weight in international affairs cannot be accomplished without its investing in International Law. - CONCERNS ON NOT UTILISING INTERNATIONAL LAW BY INDIA - Lack of use of vocabularies by India in International Law - For example, India has hesitated in calling out Chinese transgressions of India’s sovereignty. - On addressing international breach by Pakistan, India did not mention “international law” transgressions. - India has not used international courts to hold Pakistan accountable for its breach of international law. - India did not challenge Pakistan’s denial of Most Favoured Nation (MFN) status to India at the World Trade Organization. - Lack of use of International Law in conduct of diplomacy: - It has resulted in India’s failure to develop and contribute new international law doctrines, interpretations and principles that suit its national interests (except few initiatives such as the CCIT and ISA). - REASONS FOR NOT USING INTERNATIONAL LAW EFFECTIVELY - Generalist diplomats - India’s foreign service is heavily populated by generalist diplomats who are wedded to the theories of international relations. - Understaffed Legal & Treaties Division - L&T functions under Ministry of External Affairs and investigates the aspects of international law. However, the division is grossly understaffed and lawyers practising international law find it more attractive to join the government as a generalist diplomat rather than as a lawyer under the division. - Fragmentation of decision making - For example – on bilateral trade in crude oil and coal mining, Ministry of Trade & Commerce is involved along with Ministry of Petroleum, Ministry of Coal, Ministry of Environment along with Ministry of External Affairs. - Neglected Discipline - Academically, international law has largely remained a neglected discipline in the last 75 years. - Lack of Investment - Government has failed to fund research in international has further resulted in lack of investment by universities to develop International Law discipline. - MEA funds focus - The MEA funds research centres such as the Indian Council of World Affairs (ICWA). However, the ICWA focuses largely on the study of international relations, not international law. China on the other hand has poured in massive resources to build the capacity of its universities in international law, which has also benefited the Chinese government. - Weak Indian Journals - Indian Society of International Law (ISIL), established in 1959, was supposed to become a centre of excellence for law. However, ISIL has failed in producing worthwhile research in international law. Its flagship journal, the Indian Journal of International Law (IJIL), is nowhere close to the top international law journals in the world despite being over 60 years old. In contrast, the Chinese Journal of International Law launched just two decades back, is one of the top-ranked journals in the world. - Failure of academicians - International law academicians, on their part, have failed to popularise international law: This is in stark contrast to academicians in international relations and social sciences who write for the masses, not just for specialised audiences. - SUGGESTIONS - To overcome the fragmentation-related problems, a parliamentary committee report in 2016 recommended creation of a ‘Department of International Law’ under Ministry of Law & Justice. - Parliamentary committee in 2021 recommended that the MEA establish chairs for research in international law in universities. - India’s ambition of punching above its weight in international affairs cannot be accomplished without its investing in international law. - 4. POSITIONING INDIA IN CHAOTIC WORLD-INTERNATIONAL RELATIONS - There is increasing diffusion of power as a ‘rise of the rest’ leading towards an international system in which there will be no superpowers, several great powers and a lot of regional powers. A multicentre world has multiple layers of governance; regional powers and institutions enjoy a much greater significance than a bipolar or unipolar world. Hence a multipolar world defined by geopolitics provides India more choices to pursue its national interest and leadership ambitions by providing global interdependence. - TRANSFORMATION IN INDIA’S FOREIGN POLICY - India’s large and rapidly expanding economy coupled with huge population has changed the course of foreign policy in the wake of changing political and security dynamics in the post-Cold War era. - India took the path of economic liberalisation, shed its anti-West, Third-World outlook and repositioned itself in the world as an important global actor. - However, India’s position is still weak as there is absence of grand strategic thinking in India’s foreign policy in terms of long-term goals. - A test case is India’s relations with Iran which have been on backburner for some time, following a U.S. threat to impose sanctions on India if it continued to trade with Iran. Because of the freeze in relations with Iran has been high, including having to pay higher prices for crude and inability to utilise the Chabahar Connectivity Project as an alternate route to Afghanistan. - India’s status as a regional power is also contested. India is not considered as a natural leader nor a regional power of South Asia. For example, In our immediate neighbourhood, whether it be Sri Lanka or Afghanistan, India’s concerns are often not taken on the high table. - It can be concluded that ‘India is neither one of the Great Powers nor minor power; but it is one that cannot be ignored and thus, fits the most general definition of middle power which has been demonstrated by the recent steps taken by India in the recent times. - CHANGES IN INDIA’S FOREIGN POLICY - Economic Diplomacy: - Progressing with three major objectives: - Attracting foreign direct investment, Ensuring India’s participation in multilateral & regional forums and Engaging with Indian diaspora globally. - Managing Great Power Relations: - Establishing balance by signing the bilateral Logistics Exchange Memorandum Agreement (LEMOA) with the USA and simultaneously working on relations with Russia. Thus, India has continued to work on relations with both the great powers. - Countering Rise of China: - Chinese plans to capture territory in Ladakh, divert waters of Brahmaputra and CPEC has serious implications for India, with dramatic redrawing of demographic and geographical boundaries, threatening India’s territorial integrity. China had clearly emerged as a new long-term challenge. India is countering it by aligning with like-minded countries like under QUAD and undertaking projects like Project Mausam, neighbourhood first policy, Act East policy etc. - Reinvigorating Neighbourhood: - SAARC needs to be converted into an energetic regional organisation. In view of the negative role played by Pakistan within SAARC, India should encourage sub-regional cooperation. In the east, India can take steps to foster integration with Nepal, Bhutan and Bangladesh and has been doing same with BIMSTEC, BBIN, conclusion of bilateral disputes with Bangladesh etc. - Act East Policy: - India has cultivated deep ties with Japan. As Japan’s strategic goals can partly be realized by assisting in the rise of India, a benign power that would be a bulwark against unipolar Asia dominated by China, besides being a place to invest its surplus funds. - Engagement with ASEAN as a forum and bilaterally engaging with each member countries strategically. Example Changi naval base Singapore - Addressing Central Asian Republics: - Central Asia is a region critical for India’s energy, trade and security needs. There are unprecedented concerns in several Central Asian Republics about China’s increasingly dominant presence. Shanghai Cooperation Organisation (SCO) also provides India with opportunity to work with Central Asian Republics and Russia to further its own strategic interest in the region. India further wants to capture this region with initiatives like INSTC. - West Asia: - Engagement with UAE and Saudi Arabia aimed at boosting investment and credit, particularly on infrastructure and energy development. This balances, both, India’s closer relations with Israel and Pakistan’s close relation with Gulf countries. - India’s engagement with Iran is of vital significance in terms of energy, domestic and regional security imperatives. PM Modi visited Iran and signed an agreement to develop the US$500 million Chabahar port. - The Last Mile, Africa: - India has begun to look further beyond its traditional areas of concerns. There has been an active attempt to build long-term relationship with Africa, an emerging continent rich in national resources including oil and gas - WAY FORWARD - A Major overhaul is required in India’s worldview of how we interpret regional and international tensions that have increased. - The erstwhile policy of non-alignment had done little to enhance India’s image. India should move from non-alignment and to multi-alignment and all alignment. - While China today presents an acute ‘near-term problem’ for India, it is important that India does not fall into the trap that the current adversarial relationship with China is ‘carved in stone’ and can or never will be altered. - India and China are ‘civilizational’ states. The two countries may never have a very close relationship but given the history of nations there is enough scope for India to formulate a policy that would not completely close the doors on China for all time. - India’s strategic and foreign policy establishment cannot afford to overlook the nuclear aspect, given that the country is wedged between two active, and hostile, nuclear powers — China and Pakistan. India is the only state among the three, that does not see nuclear weapons as intended for use in the event of a war. Nevertheless, it is important for India’s strategic and foreign policy establishment to consider how best to prevent ‘debilitating strategic instability’ — about China in particular — given the pace at which China’s nuclear arsenal is growing. - 5. INDIA-CHINA - OVERVIEW OF INDIA - CHINA RELATIONS - In 1988, Rajiv Gandhi visited China, becoming first PM to visit after 34 years, following the vacuum caused by Sino-India war of 1962. China & India accepted that if peace and tranquility was maintained in border areas, relationship could be normalized in economic, trade, research and other areas. - All subsequent governments in India have followed same policy in dealing with China. This policy is also reflected in the India-China joint statement of 2015. - The understanding between two nations started to change with China's launch of Belt Road initiative (BRI) in 2013. It was part of China's geoeconomics and geopolitical strategy. In 2015, China launched CPEC passing through Pakistan Occupied Kashmir breaching India's sovereignty. - India highlighted that all connectivity projects must be based on internationally recognised norms and should respect territorial integrity and sovereignty. - China now opposed India's entry into Nuclear Supplier's Group, insisting that if India was admitted, Pakistan should also be treated in on similar basis. - China blocked designation Masood Azhar as terrorist under 1267 committee. - India worked closer with US and other European powers to put pressure on Pakistan via FATF grey listing. - Finally, Quad was revived in 2017 which is widely perceived as an anti-China alliance. This was followed by integration of Australia into the Malabar Naval exercise. - INDIA - CHINA BOUNDARY DISPUTE - India shares a 3488-km boundary with China along J&K, Uttarakhand, Himachal Pradesh, Sikkim & Arunachal Pradesh. - Border between India and China is not clearly demarcated throughout and there is no mutually agreed Line of Actual Control (LAC). - Entire border is divided into three sectors: - Western sector is disputed (Ladakh); Middle sector is largely settled and peaceful (Himachal Pradesh & Uttarakhand) and Eastern sector is unsettled and disputed (China claims entire Arunachal). - China claims that the border claimed by India as signed British India and Tibet was under colonial pressure and a weak China. China does not agree. - British India had failed to produce a single integrated and well-defined northern boundary separating the Indian subcontinent from Xinjiang and Tibet. -
SectorStatusDetails
Western (Ladakh)DisputedIndia accuses China of illegally occupying Aksai Chin, and some other parts of Ladakh region.
China claims Aksai Chin as extension of Tibet plateau, India claims it as extension of Ladakh plateau.
Middle (Himachal Pradesh & Uttarakhand)Largely settled and peacefulMinimal disputes, relatively stable.
Eastern (Arunachal Pradesh)Unsettled and disputedChina claims entire Arunachal Pradesh, especially Tawang, as part of Tibet.
- ON THE WESTERN SECTOR - Western sector i.e., Aksai Chin Sector - - The two sides differ over the boundary line that separates Jammu and Kashmir from Xinjiang province of China. - India accuses China of illegally occupying Aksai Chin, and some other parts of Ladakh region. - According to China, Aksai Chin is the extension of Tibet plateau whereas India claims it as an extension of Ladakh plateau. - The region is mostly uninhabited. - Aksai chin is important for China as it connects two backward provinces of China i.e., Tibet and Xinjiang. - British policy advocated farther northern Kashmiri border in the form of Johnson Line where Aksai Chin was part of Kashmir and another McDonald line under which Aksai Chin falls under Xinjiang Province of China. - As a result, the disagreement prevails with India claiming Johnson Line to be correct and China claiming McDonald Line to be correct. - In the official map of India in 1950 India marked east of Karakoram range as “Boundary Undefined”. - Currently though the LAC at present separates India and China in the absence of a mutually agreed boundary, there is a difference in perceptions about the alignment of the line. - EASTERN SECTOR- MCMAHON LINE - - Background - In eastern sector, boundary was delineated in 1914 Shimla conference of British India, China and Tibet. - Accordingly, British proposed formation of Outer-Tibet bordering India & Inner-Tiber bordering China. - A boundary demarcating Tibetan region of China and the North-east Frontier Areas of India (current Arunachal Pradesh) was agreed upon by British and Tibetan representatives which came to be called the McMahon Line. - This is another contentious issue because China does not recognize the McMahon line as it was signed between British and Tibet which was not a sovereign state at the time. - As a result, China claims Arunachal Pradesh especially Tawang as a part Tibet. - India on its part while recognizes Chinese suzerainty over Tibet and considers McMahon line to be official boundary. - INITIATIVES TO RESOLVE BORDER ISSUES - A solution to the Sino-India border dispute continues to elude the two countries after 45 rounds of talks since 1960. - Shimla agreement of 1914: - Through this agreement, McMahon Line was recognised as the legal boundary between India and China. - However, China rejects the Shimla agreement and the McMahon line, contending that Tibet was not a sovereign state and therefore did not have the power to conclude treaties. - China claims Tibet was always part of China (under Qing dynasty suzerainty), so Tibet couldn’t act as a fully independent state in 1914. China claims Arunachal because it argues Tibet historically administered it — and since Tibet is “part of China,” the area south of the McMahon Line should be too. But this claim is based on cultural links, not continuous political control, and there’s no historical Qing administration over Arunachal Pradesh. - Panchsheel Agreement of 1954: - The “Agreement on Trade and Intercourse between the Tibet region of China and India” was signed in 1954.
