Since China and Pakistan are crucial in India’s Geo-Strategic calculations, India’s engagement with NATO would add a key dimension to India’s growing engagement with the US (Quad, 2+2 dialogue) and Europe. - Engaging with NATO in a political dialogue would provide India an opportunity to bring about a balance in NATO’s perceptions about the situation in regions and issues of concerns to India. - OUTCOMES OF DIALOGUE - Convergence - There was alignment between India and NATO on China, terrorism, and Afghanistan, including Pakistan’s role in Afghanistan. - Disagreement - Common ground was not reached on the issue of Russian aggression against Ukraine. - China Perceptions - Given the economic ties of European countries with China, NATO members have mixed views, seeing China both as a challenge and an opportunity. - Afghanistan - NATO views the Taliban as a political entity, which is not aligned with India’s stance. - Maritime Security - Both India and NATO had convergence on maritime security issues. - India’s Relevance - NATO considers India relevant to international security due to its geo-strategic position and unique perspectives, making India an important partner in providing insights about its region and beyond. - CONCERNS - Cold War Relic: - NATO is seen as relic of the Cold War era where India exercised non-alignment. - Alliance Perception: - Engaging with NATO would be seen as being part of an alliance. - Expansionist Image: - Given NATO’s perception of being an expansionist block, engagement would be a setback to India’s image. - Impact on Russia: - Could impact India’s relations with Russia, given NATO’s anti-Russia stance. - WAY FORWARD - However, India has been exercising a policy of Strategic autonomy which aims at engaging with countries and groupings based on its interests. At a time when NATO is engaging with Pakistan and China, India needs cannot remain isolated. - 11. SIGNIFICANCE OF PM’S VISIT TO EUROPE - PM Modi visited three European countries – Germany, France and Denmark and participated in 2nd India – Nordic summit. At this visit, he met with 7 heads of government including the new German Chancellor Olaf Scholz, newly re-elected French President, Prime Minister of Norway, Prime Minister of Sweden, Prime Minister of Iceland, Prime Minister of Finland, and Prime Minister of Denmark. - The visit assumes importance because it was the first visit of the PM after COVID Pandemic, and it was the first visit after the war in Ukraine broke out which has considerably changed the geo-politics and geo-economics of the world. - Because of the important interactions, major geo-political events and importance that India accords to these countries, let us try and understand the key outcomes of this visit along with India’s relations with some of these countries. - IMPORTANT AGREEMENTS SIGNED/AGREED DURING THE VISIT
CountryAgreementKey Highlights
India – GermanyPartnership for Green and Sustainable DevelopmentGermany has committed to providing additional development assistance of €10 billion till 2030 to support India’s green growth plans. Agreed to a 7-point agenda on renewable energy and sustainable growth cooperation.
India – Nordic countriesEnvironmental Cooperation, Space, Cyber security, invest in India’s defence industryBoosting cooperation in wind and solar energy, pollution control and urban renewal projects and other green projects. India and Denmark have already signed a Green Strategic Partnership.
India reiterated its commitments made at COP26 in Glasgow: 500 gigawatts of non-fossil fuel by 2030, 50% of energy capacity to come from renewable energy by 2030, reduction of 1 billion tons in projected carbon emissions by 2030, carbon intensity of India’s economy to reduce by 45% by 2030 and net zero by 2070.
India – FranceGreen investments in IndiaIndia called for French investment in making India a green hydrogen hub. Both countries agreed to an integrated approach on supply chains for solar energy production in Europe and Asia.
Two sides set up a strategic dialogue on Space issues, strengthen a cyber dialogue and find more ways for France to invest in building India’s defence industry.
India – GermanyEquipment and InformationAgreed to step up high-tech transfer collaborations and signed a joint declaration of intent for an agreement on exchange of classified information.
India – NordicTechnologiesThis visit did not have a security component but focused on new technologies.
- AREAS OF DISAGREEMENT - 1. Ukraine Issue - India has refrained from taking any stance against Russia due to its own national interests. - European countries, however, expect India to denounce Russian aggression against Ukraine. - 2. Energy From Russia - The EU has only recently begun imposing a ban on Russian oil, with exceptions for Hungary and other landlocked countries. - Gas has been excluded from this ban. Meanwhile, India’s imports of Russian oil have increased significantly. - 3. India-EU FTA - The European Union is India’s third-largest trading partner after China and the US, and its second-largest export destination. - Despite its potential, a Free Trade Agreement (FTA) could further strengthen economic ties. - However, FTA negotiations have been pending since 2013 when talks were suspended. - 12. INDIA-GERMANY - PM visited Germany, France and Denmark and agreed upon various agreements. In this backdrop let us try and understand India – Germany relations in detail. - BACKGROUND OF RELATIONS - Germany is one of India’s most important partners in Europe owing to Germany’s key role in the EU. - India and Germany have a 'Strategic Partnership'. - As the 6th and 4th largest economies in the world respectively, India and Germany share a robust economic and developmental partnership. - Besides the economic interest, both countries have a shared interest in upholding democratic values, rules-based international order, multilateralism as well as the reform of multilateral institutions. - AREA OF COOPERATION - 1. Multilateral Cooperation - Both countries support each other on UNSC expansion within the G-4 framework (India, Japan, Brazil & Germany jointly pursuing permanent membership). - Germany joined the Coalition for Disaster Resilient Infrastructure (CDRI) in 2020. - Germany is a member of the International Solar Alliance (ISA) initiated by India. - Germany has aligned its Indo-Pacific strategy with India’s approach. - 2. Partnership for Green and Sustainable Development - Germany has committed additional development assistance of €10 billion till 2030 to support India’s green growth plans. - Both countries agreed on a 7-point agenda covering renewable energy and sustainable growth cooperation. - 3. Security & Defence Cooperation - MoU on Security Cooperation signed in 2015. - Dialogue Mechanisms include: - Joint Working Group on Counter Terrorism, Cyber Consultations, and Joint Steering Group on Disaster Management. - Bilateral Defence Cooperation Agreement signed in 2006. - Agreement on Mutual Protection of Classified Information signed in 2007. - Recently, both nations agreed to enhance high-tech transfer collaborations and signed a joint declaration of intent for exchanging classified information. - 4. Economic Cooperation - Germany is India’s largest trading partner in Europe and among its top ten global partners. - In FY 2020-21, Germany was India’s 7th-largest trading partner, with bilateral trade of USD 21.76 billion (about a fifth of trade between India and the US). - Germany is the 7th-largest source of FDI for India. - 5. Areas to Focus upon - Identified focus areas: - Artificial Intelligence and digitalisation, agriculture, clean energy, e-mobility, urban development, Smart Cities, railways, Industry 4.0, start-ups, skill development, water and waste management. - Both countries see mutual benefit in an India-EU Free Trade Agreement and Investment Protection Agreement, though FTA talks have been stalled since 2013. - 6. Diaspora - Approximately 2.03 lakh Indians in Germany (1.60 lakh NRIs/Indian passport holders and around 43,000 PIOs as of December 2021). - Indian diaspora primarily includes professionals, researchers, scientists, businessmen, nurses, and students. - Significant recent increase in qualified Indian professionals in IT, banking, and finance. - CONVERGENCE OF INTERESTS - 1. Pursuit of Strategic Autonomy - In a multipolar global order, both countries support each other’s pursuit of strategic autonomy. - For India, strategic autonomy means economic multi-alignment and neutrality on international conflicts. - Germany increasingly seeks strategic autonomy through the European Union. - 2. Reducing Over Dependence - In the context of the Ukraine war, Germany has been striving to reduce its dependence on Russian oil and gas. - Similarly, India recognizes its over-dependence on Russia for defence supplies. - 3. Recognition of Legitimate Interests - Evident in all agreements signed between India and Germany during PM Modi’s visit. - 4. Indo-Pacific - Germany was among the first European nations to outline Indo-Pacific guidelines in 2020. - With growing global scepticism towards China and India’s rising regional influence, more European countries are showing confidence in India to shape the strategic architecture of the region. - 5. Climate Action - Russia’s invasion of Ukraine prompted Germany to reconsider its energy import policy, creating a strategic window for Indo-German green hydrogen cooperation. - India also faces energy security challenges amidst the Ukraine-induced global crisis. - Both nations signed the Partnership for Green and Sustainable Development, reflecting close climate collaboration. - CHALLENGES IN RELATIONS - 1. De-hyphenation of India and China - European powers, including Germany, tend to de-hyphenate India and China. - This is largely due to China’s increased integration into global supply chains. - European countries are also increasingly hesitant to criticize China, as seen in their approach to Chinese expansionist policies along the Indian border. - 2. Human Rights Issues - Germany and other European countries have at times expressed concern over human rights issues in India. - 3. Trade and Investment Barriers - Germany sees trade and investment as key to strengthening ties with India. - However, a challenging business environment and stringent labour standards are considered major obstacles. - WAY FORWARD - Despite the challenges, as Germany comes to terms with new global realities, a strong partnership with India is today an important part of its foreign policy vision. This new vision has the potential to transform not only this bilateral partnership but also the wider engagement of the European Union with India. - 13. INDIA-FRANCE - Recently India and France completed 25 years of their strategic partnership (the two countries entered into strategic partnership in 1998 and the partnership between the two countries has continued to gain momentum over shared values and aspirations of peace, stability and the desire of strategic autonomy. - During the recent visit of PM Modi to France a variety of agreements chief among the agreements was the strategic road map for the next 25 years “Horizon 2047” which includes cooperation in defence, space, nuclear energy, climate change and green transitions as well as education and people-to-people ties. - The document Horizon 2047, reflects a vision of the two leaders, of Prime Minister Modi and President Macron, on where the relationship should be in 2047 when India would celebrate 100th year of its independence and we will be celebrating 50 years of our Strategic Partnership. It has 63 specific outcomes that are divided among multiple pillars. - 1. Partnership for Security and Sovereignty - A comprehensive approach to security and sovereignty cooperation between the two countries. - 2. India-France Partnership in the Indo-Pacific - Focused on providing solutions for security, strategic, and economic challenges. - 3. Cooperation in the Fight Against Terrorism - Joint efforts to combat terrorism and ensure global security. - 4. Renewed and Effective Multilateralism - Shared commitment to promoting multilateral cooperation. - 5. Science, Technology, and Academic Cooperation - Collaboration between research institutions in scientific, critical technologies, health, cyber, digital regulatory frameworks, and digital technologies. - 6. People-to-People Ties - Enhancing mobility partnership and skill-set cooperation to foster academic collaboration and meet the needs of skill development. - 7. Defence Cooperation - Roadmap for further cooperation in the Indo-Pacific, including military and naval exchanges and a trilateral development fund. - 8. Defence Procurement - Approval for the purchase of 26 Rafale-M fighter jets for the Indian Navy, three additional Scorpene submarines, and an agreement for helicopter engines. - The France-India strategic relationship is built on respect for each other's strategic autonomy, with France refraining from commenting on India's internal affairs or foreign policy choices. - AREAS OF COOPERATION - 1. Principal Pillars - The areas of defence cooperation, space cooperation and civil nuclear cooperation constitute the three principal pillars of our Strategic Partnership. - 2. Emerging Areas - Apart from these traditional fields of cooperation, India and France are increasingly engaged in new areas of cooperation like climate change, sustainable growth and development, the International Solar Alliance etc. - 3. Multipolar World Order - India and France support a multi-polar world order. - 4. UNSC Membership - France supports India’s claim for permanent membership of UN Security Council and reforms of United Nations. - 5. Export Control Regimes - France has provided consistent support to India’s candidature for the membership of all the four Multilateral Export Control regimes, viz. Nuclear Suppliers Group (NSG), the Missile Technology Control Regime (MTCR), the Wassenaar Arrangement (WA) and the Australia Group (AG). France’s support was vital in India’s accession to MTCR, WA and AG while France continues to support India’s bid for accession to the NSG. - 6. Counter-Terrorism - India and France have consistently condemned terrorism and have resolved to work together for adoption of the Comprehensive Convention on International Terrorism (CCIT) in the UN. - 7. Logistics Support - Both have signed provision of reciprocal logistics support between respective Armed Forces. - 8. Joint Visions - Both have declared Joint Statement a "Joint Strategic Vision of India-France Cooperation in the Indian Ocean Region” and the "India-France Joint Vision for Space Cooperation.” - 9. Economic Investment - France has emerged as a major source of FDI for India with more than 1,000 French establishments already present in India with a total turnover of US $20 billion and employing around 300,000 persons. France is the 9th largest foreign investor in India with a cumulative investment of USD 6.59 billion from April 2000 to December 2018. There are more than 150 Indian companies operating in France (including sub-subsidiaries), employing more than 7,000 persons. - INDIA FRANCE RELATIONS IN EMERGING GEO-POLITICAL CONTEXT - 1. Indo-Pacific - Both believe in a shared vision of a free, open and rules-based order in the Indo-Pacific, with a commitment to international law, respect for sovereignty and territorial integrity, freedom of navigation and a region free from coercion, tensions and conflicts. - 2. Ukraine Conflict - Both are divergent on the issue of taking a stand against Russia, wherein India has stayed away from condemning Russia owing to its privileged partnership with Russia while France has been critical of Russian aggression. However, both believe in the need to respect UN Charter, international law and the sovereignty and territorial integrity of states. - 3. Defence and Security - France supports India’s “Make in India” initiative and wants further transfer of technology to India. INS Vagsheer, the sixth Scorpene submarine developed at Mazagaon dock under a deal with France, is a testimony to high level of defence cooperation. Timely delivery of the Rafale despite COVID pandemic has increased optimism about defence ties between the two. - 4. Post AUKUS Opportunity - France resented the cancellation of Nuclear Submarine development program with Australia, after the formation of AUKUS, under which US will transfer the submarine technology to Australia. France, which was looking towards security partnership with Australia before the AUKUS, is now finding itself turning to India to rework its Indo-Pacific strategy. - 5. Maritime Security - India has growing maritime security concerns in the Indian Ocean given the increasing Chinese footprint in the region and its intrusions into India’s Exclusive Economic Zone in the Andamans. Given this backdrop, it is likely that India will pursue France to step up bilateral engagements but also strengthen its naval wherewithal in the region as a deterrent measure. - 6. Space Cooperation - Both the countries decided to cooperate on ways to secure outer space from several growing space security challenges including counterspace challenges. India is considering a space security dialogue mechanism with France. India has similar security dialogues with only two other countries – United States and Japan. China’s growing space and counterspace capabilities have pushed many Indo-Pacific powers to develop their own appropriate deterrent measures to protect their assets. - 7. Cyber Security - Both agree to develop a common understanding of cyber security challenges and “promoting cyber norms and principles” so as to effectively counter cyber threats and enable a “peaceful, secure and open cyberspace.” - 8. Terrorism - Both countries want to further their counterterrorism agenda by addressing several areas including financing of terrorism, radicalization and violent extremism, as well as misuse of the internet for terrorism and violent extremism. - WAY FORWARD - Given the long-standing and trusted partnership between India and France and the intensifying geopolitical trends in the Indo-Pacific region, both are likely to build an even closer partnership that will focus on building India’s military wherewithal. Even as the bilateral relationship is strong, India and France must forge more minilaterals in the Indo-Pacific, getting more countries to endorse the free and open Indo-Pacific strategy. Beyond diplomatic support and arms, France’s actual material capacity to help counter China in the Indo-Pacific remains to be seen. - 14. INDIA–JAPAN - During the recent visit PM Kishida said that today’s Ukraine could be tomorrow’s Asia,” and indicated Japan’s concerns over China’s actions in the Senkaku Islands, South China Sea and Taiwan Straits. - The issue of “shared responsibilities” on the rule of law between India and Japan was discussed by the two leaders during bilateral talks. - The Japanese Prime Minister went on to launch Japan’s New Plan for a Free and Open Indo-Pacific (FOIP). - The Japanese PM also highlighted the need for common perspective for international order. - The two leaders acknowledged that 2023 is a pivotal year for both India and Japan as chairs of the two major international groupings – India as the President of the G20 and Japan as the Chair of G7. Our countries have the unique opportunity of converging our priorities on issues of significance and drive through that and in the process, the global agenda. - The two leaders spoke about the challenge that they face in the region (China) and globally. - The two leaders further highlighted that i.e., India and Japan and other like-minded countries can work together to address those challenges. - ABOUT INDIA AND JAPAN - India and Japan celebrated 70 years of friendship in 2022. Reflecting on how this relationship has developed from a Global partnership to a “special strategic and global partnership”. - However, we can see gaps in economic partnership and people-to-people connect. While the economic partnership was the pillar on which this relationship took shape, the two leaders will unquestionably direct the policymakers of both nations to scrutinize and reflect on how to enhance this pillar. - Moreover, much-touted civilizational linkages have not translated into robust people-to-people connections. - The two leaders are hence putting forth various schemes to enable exchanges of people, namely youth, workforce, and artists, along with a stronger emphasis on tourism. - - AGREEMENTS AND MOU’S IN RECENT MEETING - 1. Mumbai-Ahmedabad High Speed Rail - The two leaders exchanged documents on the financing of the 4th tranche of Japanese funding for a loan of 300 billion Yen for the Mumbai-Ahmedabad High Speed Rail (MAHSR) or “Bullet Train” project. - 2. Japanese Language Education - A MoU on Japanese language education for the MEA. - 3. Investment Mobilization - Japan further plans to mobilise a total of U.S. $75 billion in public and private funds by 2030. - The Japanese leader also mentioned coordinating with India for projects as part of a “Bay of Bengal-Northeast India industrial value chain.” - 4. Defence Co-innovation - The two leaders also discussed co-innovation, co-design, co-creation in the defence field, and investment collaboration/partnership in this space. - Indian PM highlighted the openness of the Indian defence sector to Japanese private companies and Japanese FDI. - 5. Joint Crediting Mechanism - Ministry of Environment of Japan and Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate Change of India signed an Aide Memoire on the Joint Crediting Mechanism (JCM), which has been under discussion for quite some time between the two countries. - 6. India-Japan Year of Tourism - The two leaders announced 2023 as the “India-Japan Year of Tourism.” - Indian PM expressed the desire to declare the next year as the year of youth exchanges between the two countries. - ISSUES OF GLOBAL AND REGIONAL IMPORTANCE - 1. Four Point Plan for Ukraine - Both India and Japan have agreed that they will “not tolerate” any unilateral and forceful change in the status quo of Ukraine or any country in the world. - They will continue to push for a peaceful settlement of the conflict. - Japan and India will also jointly address the situation whenever needed. - They will work together in providing humanitarian aid and assistance to Ukraine. - 2. China LAC Stand-off - India emphasised that until and unless there is peace and tranquillity in border areas, it cannot consider business as usual with China. - Japan raised the matter of East China Sea and South China Sea regarding China’s growing belligerence there. - Emphasis on non-militarisation and self-restraint, prioritising international law, particularly the United Nations Convention on the Law of the Sea (UNCLOS). - Facilitate collaboration, including maritime security, to meet challenges against the rules-based maritime order in the East and South China Seas. - 3. Japanese Investments in India - $42 billion in the next 5 years to finance public and private projects of mutual interest. - FOUNDING PILLARS OF INDIA – JAPAN RELATIONS - STRATEGIC COLLABORATION BETWEEN INDIA AND JAPAN - Indo-Pacific - Convergence on free, open and inclusive Indo-Pacific. - Defence and Security - Defence and security in the region – Quad, AUKUS, MALABAR. - India and Japan signed a Reciprocal Provision of Supplies and Services Agreement (RPSS). - The inaugural 2+2 ministerial meeting was held in November 2019. - Act East Forum - A decision was taken in the 2017 Summit to establish the India-Japan Act East Forum. - Objective: - Coordinate developmental projects in North-East India in areas of connectivity, forest management, disaster risk reduction, and capacity building. - Northeast Development - Upgradation of highways in Meghalaya, Tripura, and Mizoram underway. - 20 km-long bridge over the Brahmaputra River between Assam and Meghalaya. - Supply Chain Resilience Initiative (SCRI) - Launched by Trade and Economy Ministers of India, Japan, and Australia on 27 April 2021. - ECONOMIC COLLABORATION - Investment Promotion Partnership 2014 - Achieved the target of 3.5 trillion Japanese Yen in public and private investments in India. - Industrial Collaboration - 1,455 Japanese companies in India. - Eleven Japan Industrial Townships (JIT). - Foreign Direct Investment (FDI) and Official Development Assistance (ODA) - Japan is the 5th largest source of FDI and largest supplier of ODA to India. - Infrastructure Projects - Projects include Mumbai-Ahmedabad High Speed Rail, Dedicated Freight Corridor, metro projects, DMIC, etc. - Digital Partnership 2018 - Indian start-ups raised over USD 10 billion from Japanese VCs. - Private sector-driven fund-of-funds launched to invest in technology start-ups in India, raised USD 100 million. - ICT Cooperation - Collaboration in ICT, including 5G, under-sea cables, telecom, and network security. - Movement of Skilled Workers - Japan’s working-age population is declining; India can supply skilled workers. - MoU on Specified Skilled Workers to promote movement from India to Japan on a contractual basis. - Program enhancements: - Demand-supply matching using digital platforms, more test centers, awareness campaigns, Japanese language promotion, program monitoring, focus on North-East India. - Skill Development - Japan-India Institutes of Manufacturing (JIM) now 19. - Established by Japanese companies in India to train skilled workers. - COLLABORATION IN THE INDO–PACIFIC - Regional Collaboration - Collaboration in QUAD, SRI, MALABAR naval exercises with US and other like-minded countries. - Countering Chinese Assertion - Cooperation along LAC, South China Sea, and East China Sea. - Free and Open Indo-Pacific (FOIP) and Act East - Japan's FOIP Strategy and India's Act East Policy converge in northeast India. - Example: - Act EAST Forum. - Third Country Collaboration - Infrastructure development in third countries like Sri Lanka, Myanmar, and Bangladesh. - Asia Africa Growth Corridor (AAGC) - Collaborative effort to soft-balance China’s Belt and Road Initiative (BRI). - COLLABORATION ON GLOBAL ISSUES - Rules-Based Order - Both believe in upholding freedom of navigation and reinforcing rules-based international order. - NSG Support - Japan supported a waiver of NSG embargo on nuclear trade with India and backed its application for membership. - Joint Military Exercises - Exercises like JIMEX, MALABAR contribute to the image of rising regional power. - Global Issues - Cooperation on proliferation of Weapons of Mass Destruction (WMDs), terrorism, space and cyber security, environment (e.g., International Solar Alliance). - United Nations Security Council (UNSC) Membership - Both are members of G4 club (India, Japan, Brazil, Germany), supporting permanent UNSC membership. - 15. INDIA AND AUSTRALIA - Prime Minister Narendra Modi’s visit to Australia. The visit, which was originally planned for a multilateral event, the meeting of the Quad, it transformed into a purely bilateral visit after the U.S. President pulled out over domestic political constraints; Japanese Prime Minister Fumio Kishida followed suit, and a shortened Quad Summit was held in Hiroshima. As a result, PM visit to Australia was much more in the spotlight. - It needs to be highlighted that that purpose of such visits is conducive to strengthening the common understanding between both countries, or in the best interests of the “three D’s” i.e.—Democracy, Diaspora and Dosti [Friendship] as earlier reiterated by PM Modi. - During the recent visit the discussions focused in multiple areas with areas covering like that of cooperation in defence and security, trade and investment, new and renewable energy, green hydrogen, critical minerals, education, migration and mobility and people to people ties. - Institutionalisation of India-Australia Summit: - India & Australia has upgraded their relationship to Annual bilateral summits at the highest political level in 2023. A Consulate General of India in Brisbane was also established highlighting the growing trust, convergence of interests among the two middle powers. - India & Australia have already raised their relationship status to India-Australia Comprehensive Strategic Partnership level. - Strategic aspect of relations: - The leaders also reiterated their determination to ensure a peaceful, prosperous and inclusive Indo-Pacific region, underpinned by a rules-based international order. They also discussed reform of UN Security Council. Australian leader also expressed strong support to India's G20 Presidency and initiatives. - Economic aspect of relations: - Australia has many expatriate Indians to contribute to its economy. Also, it is a place where large number of Indian students go for higher education and employment. In this respect, the signing of India-Australia Migration & Mobility Partnership Agreement will further facilitate mobility of students, professionals, researchers, academics. This will be done through a new skilled pathway named MATES (Mobility Arrangement for Talented Early Professionals Scheme) specifically created for India. - A Business Roundtable with CEOs of top Australian companies was also organised, and the business leaders were invited to invest in India in areas particularly including that of digital infrastructure, IT, fintech, telecom, semiconductors, space, renewable energy including green hydrogen, education, pharma, healthcare including medical devices manufacturing, mining including critical minerals, textile, agriculture & food processing. - Partnership in emerging technologies: - The two countries have also finalized Terms of Reference of the India-Australia Hydrogen Task Force, to focus on deployment of clean hydrogen, fuel cells etc highlighting the cooperation in areas beyond traditional spheres of diplomacy. - However, despite of the increasing cooperation between the two countries there are still deep challenges that exist between two countries. - Significance Area Outcomes of Cooperation - This data was in table with three columns - Economic - Australia is an important trade partner of India with bilateral trade around $25bn. - Double Taxation Avoidance Agreement (DTAA) signed to avoid double taxation of offshore income of Indian companies doing business in Australia. - Australia can be a key partner in India’s “Make-in-India” initiative. - Investment opportunities for Indian companies in Australian mining and energy sectors. - India’s infrastructure sector under National Investment and Infrastructure Fund (NIIF) can offer investment opportunities to Australian funds, including pension funds. - Explored possibility of launching Indian RuPay Card in Australia. - Maritime - Shared vision- free, open, inclusive, rules-based Indo-Pacific region. - Common concerns on strategic, security, and environmental challenges in Indo-Pacific maritime domain. - Australia granted Rs 8 crore to India’s Indo-Pacific Ocean Initiative (IPOI). - Australian Navy actively participates in Indian Ocean Naval Symposium (IONS), started by India. - Defence - Australia is a key defence partner of India. - Increasing defence ties are crucial given China’s expansionism on land and maritime fronts. - Signed Mutual Logistic Service Agreement (MLSA) for reciprocal access to military logistics facilities. - Signed “White Shipping Agreement.” - Conducts bilateral maritime exercise AUS-INDEX once every 2 years. - Australia participated in Malabar Naval Exercise in 2020. - Energy - Australia is rich in coal, uranium, and natural gas reserves, aiding India’s energy security. - Signed Civil Nuclear Cooperation Agreement. - Explicit support for India’s membership in Nuclear Suppliers Group (NSG), despite India being a non-signatory of NPT. - Strategic and Multilateral Cooperation - Both countries share membership in the QUAD. - Commitment to multilateralism. - Australia supports UNSC reforms and India’s candidature for permanent membership. - People-to-People - Strong Indian diaspora in Australia. - Indian diaspora is one of the largest sources of skilled migrants. - India is the 2nd largest source of international students in Australia. - A “Migration and Mobility Partnership Arrangement” is being designed to: - Prevent illegal migration and human trafficking. - Facilitate mobility of students and researchers. - Promote migration of professionals for economic purposes. - Mutual Logistics Support Agreement (MLSA) - The agreement will facilitate reciprocal access to military logistics facilities and improve interoperability between the security forces of the two nations. - This will be useful during Bilateral naval exercises and Humanitarian, Disaster relief & rescue operation. - White Shipping Agreement - It refers to exchange of relevant advance information on the identity and movement of commercial non-military merchant vessels. Being aware of the identity of these vessels is imperative to preventing any potential threat from the sea from impinging on the coastal and offshore security of the country. - CHALLENGES IN THE RELATIONSHIP - Dichotomous Australian Foreign Policy - Misalignment in Australia’s economic and political interests. - Though Australia actively engages with India and US as part of QUAD, its economy still heavily depends on China due to large bilateral trade and investment. - Lingering CECA - Comprehensive Economic Cooperation Agreement (CECA) has not materialised despite negotiations starting in 2011. - This remains a hindrance to bilateral trade. - Withdrawal from Regional Comprehensive Economic Partnership (RCEP) - One reason for India’s withdrawal from RCEP was objections from farmer organisations and dairy cooperatives. - Fears of flooding of cheaper agricultural and dairy products from Australia were cited. - INDO-PACIFIC GEOPOLITICS & INDIA – AUSTRALIA RELATIONSHIP - Chinese Belligerence: - India and Australia have had to endure increasingly belligerent Chinese behaviour in the Indo-Pacific, pushing their bilateral relationship to new heights. - Improving Trajectory: - Australia-India relations have been on an improving trajectory in recent years. - Malabar Naval Exercise: - Australia was invited back to the Malabar naval exercise last year after many years. - AUKUS and Asian Partnerships: - While the U.S. security alliance (AUKUS) remains central to Australia, it has been expanding its Asian partnerships in the Indo-Pacific. - India’s Strategic Shift: - India long avoided taking sides between the U.S. and China, but the Galwan confrontation in 2020 brought Sino-Indian relations to an all-time low. India is now pursuing closer strategic engagements with like-minded partners, especially Australia. - Chinese Coercion: - Australia has faced bullying behaviour from China, largely due to its request for an independent inquiry into the origins of COVID-19. - Economic Coercion: - China has engaged in severe trade coercion against Australia, targeting barley, beef, seafood, wheat, wine, coal, cotton, copper, education, and timber. - Augmented Ties: - Bilateral, trilateral, quadrilateral, and other minilateral and multilateral engagements have strengthened India-Australia ties. - Strategic Convergence: - Shared interests in ensuring a free Indo-Pacific order and initiatives like the Supply Chain Resilience Initiative (SCRI) further bind the two countries. - 16. FREE TRADE AGREEMENTS - India pushing forward the long pending FTAs. The aim is to achieve the $2 trillion export target by 2030, and address disruptions in global supply chains created by the pandemic and the ongoing Russia-Ukraine war. - ABOUT FTAs: - Definition: - Free trade pacts remove entry barriers for goods & services between two trading partners. - Trade Conditions: - Goods & services can be bought and sold across international borders with minimal or no government tariffs, quotas, or subsidies. - Recent Developments: - India resumed talks with EU and Australia, started talks with UK, and finalised FTA with UAE. The India-UAE FTA is expected to double bilateral trade to $100 billion in 5 years while eliminating tariffs on a range of products. - AI ECTA: - India and Australia signed the first part of Australia-India Economic Cooperation & Trade Agreement (AI ECTA). - Early Harvest Deals: - India focuses on signing agreements on easier aspects first, keeping main tariff eliminations for later. - Pipeline FTAs: - Agreements with Israel, Canada, New Zealand, Indonesia, and Thailand are in the pipeline. - WHY A RENEWED PUSH FOR FTAs? - Past Reluctance: - Previous governments avoided FTAs fearing increased imports. - Strategic Alignment: - India seeks to enhance economic ties with strategic partners like Australia, France, and the UK. - Absence from Mega-Trade Pacts: - India is outside mega-trade pacts like RCEP; now entering minilaterals like I2U2, IPEF & Quad, which focus on supply chains but don’t guarantee market access. - Export-Led Growth: - FTAs are key for export-led growth, forex earnings, and job creation. - Market Access: - India wants greater access to complementary economies, since WTO negotiations are slow. - Reduce Imports from China: - To curb rising imports from China despite border clashes. - Rising Oil and Food Prices: - India aims to strengthen trade with friendly countries amid global price shocks from the Ukraine war. - CHALLENGES AND HURDLES: - Tariff Reduction: - Partner countries demand reduction in import tariffs for market access. - Domestic Industry Exposure: - Domestic industries face high competition and risk losing market share. - High Tariff Rates: - India’s average tariff is 15%, highest in Asia-Pacific, up from 13.5% in 2016. - RCEP Withdrawal: - India exited RCEP due to domestic industry pressure. - Australian Agricultural Tariffs: - Australia seeks major tariff cuts on agricultural produce, wine, and spirits, opposed by India. - UK Whisky Tariffs: - UK demands lower tariffs on whisky under India-UK FTA talks. - EU Demands: - EU will seek tariff eliminations in automobiles and auto parts. - Early Harvest Reluctance: - UK & EU are hesitant to finalise early harvest deals. - Aatmanirbhar Bharat Concerns: - Emphasis on self-reliance has made partner countries wary. - CHAPTER 5 - INTERNATIONAL ORGANISATIONS - 1. WTO – GENEVA PACKAGE - Under its 12th Ministerial Conference, WTO members agreed to a series of deals relating to temporary waivers on Covid-19 vaccines, a moratorium on e-commerce trade, food security and setting limits on harmful fishing subsidies. - IMPORTANCE OF MINISTERIAL CONFERENCE - Topmost decision-making body of WTO is the Ministerial Conference. - Usually meets every two years. - Brings together all members of the WTO, all of which are countries or customs unions. - Ministerial Conference can take decisions on all matters under any of multilateral trade agreements. - GENEVA PACKAGE - HIGHLIGHTS OF A HISTORIC MC-12 OUTCOME PACKAGE - TRADE AND HEALTH - Members agree on a COVID-19 global response, a framework to render WTO more resilient & better prepared for future pandemics. - AGRICULTURE - Declaration: - Draft Ministerial Declaration on the emergency response to food insecurity. - WFP Exemption: - Ministerial Decision to exempt food purchased by the UN's World Food Programme (WFP) for humanitarian purposes from any export restrictions. - FISHERIES SUBSIDIES - In a historic milestone for ocean sustainability, WTO members have foged an Agreement on Fisheries Subsidies which sets new global rules to curb harmful subsidies and protect global fish stocks for all those who rely on it, in a manner that also recognizes the needs of developing and least-developed countries. Members also commit to continue negotiations, including for further rules on certain fisheries subsidies that contribute to overcapacity and overfishing. - E-COMMERCE - WTO members agreed to maintain the current practice of not imposing customs duties on electronic transmissions until MC13. - DECISION ON AGRICULTURE - Member countries agreed to a binding decision to exempt food purchased by the UN’s World Food Program (WFP) for humanitarian purposes, from any export restrictions. - BACKGROUND: - Global Food Crisis: - Global food crisis because of Russia-Ukraine war. - India's Wheat Ban: - India's export ban on wheat. - Wheat Exporters: - Russia and Ukraine are amongst top wheat exporters in the world. - Export Share: - Five biggest wheat exporters (Russia, USA, Australia, Canada, and Ukraine) provided about three-fifths (59.5%) of overall value of international shipments for the nourishing cereal food. - Impact: - Exports have been adversely impacted because of the war, affecting food security in many countries (e.g., Egypt and other MENA countries). - India's Ban: - India, due to rising inflation, put a ban on wheat exports. - IMPACT: - Developed Countries: - Wanted a blanket ban on export restrictions and prohibitions. - Developing Countries: - Against such blanket ban to ensure domestic food security. - Outcome: - The agreement will ensure availability, accessibility, and affordability of food to those in need, especially in humanitarian emergencies. Member countries would not impose export prohibitions or restrictions on foodstuffs purchased for humanitarian purposes of the WFP. Encouraged member countries with available surplus to release them on international markets in compliance with WTO regulations. Instituted a work program to help LDCs (least-developed countries) and NFIDCs (Net Food Importing Developing Countries) enhance domestic food security and bolster agricultural production. - Unresolved Issue: - Could not reach agreements on issues such as permissible public stockholding threshold for domestic food security, domestic support to agriculture, cotton, and market access. India’s key demand to allow it to export food from its public stockholdings to other countries will be discussed in the next Ministerial Conference in 2023. - PUBLIC STOCKHOLDING ISSUE: - India’s Demand: - India has been demanding a permanent solution on Public Stockholding to implement National Food Security Act. - Bali Ministerial 2013: - At the Bali ministerial conference in December 2013, India secured a “peace clause." Under it, if India breaches the 10% limit on subsidy under AoA, other member countries will not take legal action under the WTO dispute settlement mechanism. Further, in 2014, India forced developed countries to clarify that the peace clause will continue indefinitely until a permanent solution is found. - AGREEMENT ON HARMFUL FISHERIES SUBSIDIES - WTO passed a multilateral agreement that would curb ‘harmful’ subsidies on illegal, unreported and unregulated fishing for the next four years, to better protect global fish stocks. - Agreement on Fishery Subsidies: - The WTO member countries were negotiating a multilateral treaty of Fishery Subsidies. This agreement seeks to prohibit certain forms of fisheries subsidies that contribute to overcapacity and overfishing. Some developed countries such as USA have been insisting that larger developing countries like India and China should not continue to get special and differential treatment. However, India has argued that special and differential treatment should be built into the fisheries subsidy agreement. - HARMFUL FISHING SUBSIDIES: - Definition: - Most ‘harmful’ fishing subsidies are those aimed at enhancing the fishing capacity of a vessel, including fuel subsidies, which make long-distance fishing economically viable. These subsidies are often relied upon by developing countries as well as developed countries. - Impact: - Harmful fisheries subsidies unbalance the fishing industry, incentivizing vessels to catch and remove fish faster than stocks can replenish. - UN FAO Data: - UN FAO has reported that 34% of world’s assessed fish resources are overfished, steadily increasing since the 1970s, when it was only around 10%. - OUTCOME: - UN SDG Alignment: - Agreement on harmful fisheries subsidies is in line with UN Sustainable Development Goal 14.6 (UN SDG). - Prohibitions: - Includes a strong prohibition of subsidies contributing to illegal, unregulated, and unreported (IUU) fishing with unprecedented transparency provisions. Also includes an absolute prohibition of subsidies for fishing on unregulated high seas. - Trust Fund: - A dedicated trust fund to provide technical assistance and capacity building for developing countries to implement the agreement. - Concession to Developing Countries: - The only exception for continuing subsidies for overfished stock is when they are deemed essential to rebuild them to a biologically sustainable level. There would be no limitation on subsidies granted or maintained by developing or least-developed countries for fishing within their exclusive economic zones (EEZ). This exception is provided because many people in developing and LDCs participate in small-scale fishing and rely on fish as a source of protein. - Significance: - First-time trade issues have been balanced with environmental issues under the aegis of WTO. Livelihood issues in developing and least-developed countries have been considered. A step in the direction of sustainable utilisation of fish stocks to boost food security. - MORATORIUM ON ELECTRONIC TRANSMISSIONS - WTO members agreed to extend moratorium on not imposing customs duties on electronic transmission (ET) until next ministerial conference. - Developed countries like USA, U.K., Australia, Japan and China etc., had sought an extension of the moratorium. - Developing countries led by India and South Africa were against extension of the moratorium. - ABOUT ELECTRONIC TRANSMISSIONS: - Definition: - ETs consist of online deliveries such as music, e-books, films, software, and video games. They differ from other cross-border e-commerce since they are ordered online but not delivered physically. - Developed Countries’ Position: - US, UK, Australia, Japan, China, etc., sought an extension of the moratorium. This would help maintain certainty and predictability for businesses and consumers, particularly during the pandemic. - Developing Countries’ Position: - Developing countries including India and South Africa opposed the moratorium. They wanted clearance on imposition of customs duties on such transmissions to generate more revenues and facilitate investment. They submitted that extending duty-free market access due to the moratorium resulted in a global loss of $10 billion per annum, 95% of which was borne by developing countries. - Why Impose Custom Duties?: - Customs duties are traditionally used to avert an undesired surge in imports, allowing nascent domestic industries to remain competitive. They provide necessary capital infusion for capacity building and help address the digital divide, particularly in low-income and developing countries, worsened by the COVID-19 pandemic. - Outcome: - Agreed to extend the moratorium on not imposing customs duties on electronic transmissions (ET) until the next ministerial conference. - E-COMMERCE ISSUES: - Developed Countries’ Proposals: - Developed countries led by the USA proposed tackling barriers that prevent cross-border sales, addressing forced data localization requirements, and permanently banning customs duties on electronic transmissions, among others. - India’s Position: - India has clearly stated that it is against any binding rules in e-commerce. - PATENT RELAXATION OF COVID VACCINES - Agreement: - Agreed on authorising the use of patents for producing COVID-19 vaccines by a member country without the consent of the rights holder for 5 years. Member countries were asked to waive requirements, including export restrictions under WTO regulations, to supply domestic markets and other countries with vaccines. WTO members to decide within six months whether to extend the waiver to therapeutics and diagnostics. Aim: improve vaccine equity, affordability, and accessibility. - Demand of Developing Countries: - India and South Africa proposed the patent waiver to speed up COVID-19 vaccine production for developing countries. Critics note that the waiver currently covers only vaccines, with a six-month timeline to decide on extension to diagnostics and therapeutics. - Opposition by Developed Countries: - Pharmaceutical companies in developed countries argued that the waiver could hurt future innovation and would not lead to faster vaccine production. - Waiver Details: - Any pharmaceutical company can manufacture vaccines or medicines without patents or voluntary licensing agreements. Companies have freedom to reverse-engineer, set prices, and export without limitations. They are not liable to compensate patent holders. Such waivers under WTO treaties (e.g., TRIPS) require consensus at the WTO Ministerial Conference and are only granted in exceptional circumstances like the COVID-19 pandemic. - WTO DECISION ON HANDLING FUTURE PANDEMICS - MC outcome contains a specific rebuke and reminder to the developed countries of their commitment under TRIPS Agreement, which urges them to encourage technology transfer from their countries to the Least Developed Countries. - Includes a series of issues members should consider preparing for future pandemics like export restrictions, trade facilitation, regulatory cooperation, and transparency, amongst others. - BASICS OF WTO - Definition: - Provides developing countries special rights and allows other members to treat them more favorably. Includes longer time periods for implementing agreements, support to build infrastructure for WTO work, and higher subsidies compared to developed countries (e.g., under Agreement on Agriculture, AoA). - Multilateral Agreements: - Adopted through consensus among all WTO members. Provisions apply to all members but may incorporate S&D for developing economies. Examples: AoA, GATS, TRIPS. - Plurilateral Agreements: - Agreements between a limited number of WTO members on a voluntary basis. Provisions apply only to signatories. Examples: Trade in civil aircraft, Government Procurement, Bovine meat, Dairy products, ITA-1 & II. - 2. DOHA ROUND - Aim to achieve major reform of international trading system through introduction of lower trade barriers and revised trade rules. The work program covers about 20 areas of trade. - It was an important step in prioritizing public health over intellectual property rights in certain situations. - The Round is also known as Doha Development Agenda as a fundamental objective is to improve the trading prospects of developing countries. - IMPORTANT DECLARATIONS UNDER DOHA ROUND - TRIPS and Public Health: - Reaffirmed flexibility of TRIPS member states to circumvent patent rights for better access to essential medicines. - Implementation Issues: - Refers to problems raised by developing countries regarding implementation of current WTO agreements from the Uruguay Round. - Agriculture: - Members committed to negotiations for improved market access, reduction of export subsidies, and substantial reduction of trade-distorting domestic support. S&DT for developing countries is integral to negotiations, addressing development needs including food security and rural development. - Special and Differential Treatment (S&DT): - Agreed that S&DT provisions are integral to WTO agreements and will be reviewed to make them more precise, effective, and operational. - Non-Agricultural Market Access (NAMA): - Covers trade negotiations on industrial products. Focus on reducing/eliminating customs tariffs and non-tariff barriers, including tariff peaks, high tariffs, and tariff escalation. Remains inconclusive due to lack of consensus. - 3. FAILURE OF DOHA ROUND - After Two decades the Doha Round of WTO is yet to achieve its goals. - Before 2003 Cancun ministerial, G20 was formed which included major developing agri-producing countries. Thus, the developing countries tried to push for agriculture issues which were not accepted by US and developed countries. - Talks failed in 2008 on the issue of Special Safeguard Mechanism (SSM) in Agriculture. Developing countries want that they should be allowed to raise tariffs temporarily to deal with import surges or price falls. - Bali Ministerial 2003 (Trade Facilitation agreement) was passed which was implemented in 2017. This agreement fulfils fundamental objective of cutting down red tape, expediting the movement, release and clearance of goods. It also reflects in full the S&DT applicable for developing and least-developed countries. - Agriculture remained an unresolved issue at Bali 2013. - Public stockholding issue: - At Uruguay Round, Public stockholding limit was fixed at, subsidies (difference between administered price (MSP in India) and market price) being no more than 10% of the value of production of the commodity. fixed external reference price was based on 1986-88 rates and there was no provision for inflation. - India with support of G33 coalition of countries wanted to move public stockholding to Green Box Subsidies (Permissible subsidies) to ensure Food Security under the National Food Security Act 2013. Developed countries opposed it. - In the end, compromise was that there would be a peace clause — India and others who avail of public stockholding will not be dragged to dispute settlement until a permanent solution is found. - This accompanied with the US’s insistence on the definition of developing countries. - Failure of appointments to appellate body because US blocked the appointments. - 4. INFORMATION TECHNOLOGY AGREEMENT (ITA) - INFORMATION TECHNOLOGY AGREEMENT (ITA-1) - Information Technology Agreement (ITA) is a plurilateral agreement under the WTO which came into force in 1997. It presently covers 81 WTO member countries which account for approximately 97% of world trade in information technology products. Every member country signing this agreement is required to eliminate tariffs on IT products listed in the Annex A and Annex B of the IT Agreement. Some IT products covered in this agreement include computers laptops, mobile phones, set up boxes, semiconductors, telecommunication equipment and parts etc. India is a signatory to ITA-1 and hence it has eliminated customs duties on 217 IT products over a period. - INFORMATION TECHNOLOGY AGREEMENT (ITA-2) - In 2015, some member countries agreed to expand the products covered by the Information Technology Agreement by eliminating tariffs on an additional list of 201 products. This was done on account of new advances in the field of information technology. The new accord covers new generation semi-conductors, semi-conductor manufacturing equipment, optical lenses, GPS navigation equipment, and medical equipment such as magnetic resonance imaging products and ultra-sonic scanning apparatus. However, it is to be noted that India has not signed ITA-2 since it would have an adverse impact on domestic manufacturing due to cheaper imports. - PRESENT CONTROVERSY - India’s Viewpoint: - Elimination of customs duties under ITA-1 hurt domestic IT component manufacturing. To boost local production, customs duties were raised to ~20% in Union Budget 2018-19. India asserts these goods fall under ITA-2, to which it is not a signatory, so no obligation exists to reduce duties. - WTO Member Countries’ Viewpoint: - Concerned about India’s customs duty hike on IT products, alleging that these products fall under ITA-1, not ITA-2. - 5. BREAKDOWN IN WTO DISPUTE SETTLEMENT MECHANISM - The WTO appellate body has become defunct after USA consistently blocked the appointment of Judges to the appellate body. The break down in the dispute settlement mechanism is a huge blow to the role of WTO which is facing the threat of trade war and rising protectionist policies of developed economies. - UNDERSTANDING DISPUTE SETTLEMENT MECHANISM - Settling disputes is the responsibility of the Dispute Settlement Body (DSB) which consists of all WTO members. - First stage: - Consultation (up to 60 days) to settle the trade disputes through conciliation. - Second stage (up to 1 year): - Failure of consultations leads to formation of Dispute Panel by the DSB. The report of the panel can be rejected only through consensus among the members of the DSB. - Appeal Stage: - Either side can appeal a panel’s ruling. Each appeal is heard by three members of a permanent seven-member Appellate Body set up by the Dispute Settlement Body. The members of the Appellate Body have four-year terms. The appeal can uphold, modify or reverse the panel’s legal findings and conclusions. The Dispute Settlement Body has to accept or reject the appeals report and rejection is only possible by consensus. - PRESENT CONTROVERSY - Sanctioned Strength: - 7 members appointed by consensus; quorum of 3 judges required to hear a case. - US Blocking Appointment: - US claims AB is biased and has blocked member appointments. Since Dec 10, 2019, only 1 judge remains, making the AB dysfunctional. - 6. AGREEMENT ON AGRICULTURE - Agreement on Agriculture (AoA) aims to facilitate international trade in agricultural goods by putting a cap on the agricultural subsidies given by the member countries. This agreement stands on 3 pillars viz. Domestic Support, Market Access, and Export Subsidies. - - CURRENT ISSUES WITH THE AOA: - National Food Security Act: - To implement NFSA, government needs to procure more food grains via MSP, which may exceed AoA subsidy limits. Developed countries like the USA challenge this. - Bali Ministerial 2013: - India secured a “peace clause” allowing temporary breach of 10% subsidy limit without legal action. India now demands a permanent solution on Public Stockholding for NFSA. - INDIA'S ARGUMENT AGAINST SUBSIDIES UNDER AOA: - Deceptive Subsidy Limit: - Absolute subsidies by developed countries are much higher than India’s despite percentage limits. - No Inflation Adjustment: - Subsidy limits are based on 1986-88 production values; inflation has increased agricultural commodity prices since. - Green Box Misuse: - Developed countries like USA misuse Green Box (direct income support not linked to product), with ~50% of US agricultural production value supported this way. - Food Security Procurement: - MSP procurement is for domestic food security, not export boosting; should be classified under Green Box rather than Amber Box. - Conclusion: - India seeks to correct historical imbalances and ensure fair, rule-based international trade under AoA. - WTO - In June 2022, the member-countries of the World Trade Organization (WTO) managed to hammer out a face-saving deal, India played a vital role at the Geneva ministerial conference, thereby keeping faith in trade multilateralism alive. - An important part was resurrecting the WTO’s dispute settlement system (DSS), also called WTO’s ‘crown jewel’, by 2024. Since 2019, the WTO’s two-tiered DSS remains paralysed. - The appellate body, which is the second tier of the WTO’s DSS that hears appeals from WTO panels, is non-functional because the United States, single-handedly, has blocked the appointment of its members. - The U.S. reproaches the appellate body for judicial overreach and exceeding its assigned institutional mandate. - One major problem that the U.S. identifies is that the appellate body, contrary to the text of the WTO’s dispute settlement understanding (DSU), has been creating binding precedents through its decisions. - 7. UN AND RETREAT OF MULTILATERALISM - United Nations commemorated its 75th anniversary on September 21, 2020 by adopting a Declaration. The anniversary comes at a time when the world is witnessing a retreat from multilateralism. - Multilateralism: - In international relations, multilateralism refers to an alliance of multiple countries pursuing a common goal. - HOW DOES IT HELP? - Bind Powerful Nations: - Binds powerful nations to avoid possibility of future conflict. - Discourage Unilateralism: - Discourages unilateralism. - Voice for Small Powers: - Gives small powers a voice and influence that they could not otherwise exercise; small countries can collectively influence larger ones. - Influence Among Great Powers: - Multilateralism allows great powers to influence each other through negotiation rather than costly bilateral control. - Post-WWII Adoption: - Multilateralism adopted post-WWII via UNO, though its importance is eroding due to challenges. - CHALLENGES TO MULTILATERALISM: - Rise of Nationalistic Politics: - Seen in global and emerging powers; e.g., Brexit. - Powerful Nations: - Challenging the multilateral world order. - Rise in Conflicts: - Conflicts in Syria, Yemen, Afghanistan show lack of collaboration among major powers. - Changing Geopolitics: - Rise of China, Russia-China axis, SCO; West seeks to maintain hegemony, leading to erosion of cooperation. - Lack of Consensus: - Cross-national issues like poverty, hunger, climate change require collaboration; zero-sum attitudes cause sub-optimal outcomes. - CHALLENGES TO UN SYSTEM: - Cold War: - First challenge; world divided into two camps. - Post-Cold War: - NATO and US containment of Russia; China uses P5 status against designating Pakistan-based terrorists. - VIOLATION OF THE CHARTER – UKRAINE WAR: - UN Principles: - Territorial integrity and sovereignty of states are UN principles. - Russia’s Veto: - Russia vetoed resolution on Ukraine. - U.S. Veto: - US vetoed resolution condemning Israel’s actions in occupied territories. - Veto Misuse: - P5 members use veto based on interests, not UN principles. - LACK OF CONSENSUS ON NUCLEAR DISARMAMENT: - Nuclear Security Perception: - Ukraine war shows nuclear weapons perceived as security guarantee. - Case Studies: - Iran and North Korea avoided war; Ukraine and Libya, which gave up nuclear weapons, were invaded. - NPT Issues: - Nuclear states used NPT to perpetuate their weapons while non-nuclear states rely on guarantees. - Impact: - Actions may increase nuclear arms race. - CHALLENGES IN THE FUNCTIONING OF UN AND OTHER MULTILATERAL BODIES: - UNSC Issues: - Rift among P5, lack of representation, failure on emerging conflicts. - UNGA Limitations: - More representative but less powerful than UNSC. - WHO Failures: - Failed early warnings during COVID-19. - WTO Failures: - Doha Agenda negotiations stalled; bilateralism and protectionism rising. - Lack of Accountability: - Violators of international law not held accountable. - Geo-Political Interests: - Nations pursue narrow interests (e.g., UNESCO funding issues in 2019). - Leadership Bias: - USA & EU dominate World Bank and IMF leadership; poor countries have limited influence. - Arms Control: - Threatened by dismantling Iran nuclear deal; climate efforts mostly symbolic. - Internet Governance: - Private companies hoard data; authoritarian states misuse it for surveillance. - ISSUES IN FINANCING OF UN: - Peacekeeping Budget: - 95,000 personnel engaged, but budget just $8 billion; troop-contributing countries rarely decide mandates. - Outstanding Contributions: - $711 million unpaid in general budget. - Voluntary Contributions: - Humanitarian, development, and agency budgets rely heavily on voluntary funds. - Climate Change Financing: - Developed nations failed to deliver $100 bn promised for climate finance to developing countries. - 8. REFORMED MULTILATERALISM - In line with its agenda in its 8th term as the non-permanent member of the UNSC, India has reaffirmed its commitment to New Orientation for Reformed Multilateral System or NORMS at the UN Economic and Social Council meeting. So let us understand the need for reforming present paradigm of Multilateralism. - BACKGROUND- NORMS - India’s entry into UNSC aims to push for reformed multilateralism under NORMS framework. - 5-Fold Objective of NORMS: - 1. Finding new opportunities for progress - 2. Effective response to international terrorism - 3. Reforming multilateral systems - 4. Comprehensive approach to international peace and security - 5. Technology with a human touch - APPROACH - India’s strategy for reformed multilateralism rests on 5S pillars: - 1. Samman (Respect) - 2. Samvad (Dialogue) - 3. Sahyog (Cooperation) - 4. Shanti (Peace) - 5. Samriddhi (Prosperity) - NEED FOR REFORM IN MULTILATERAL WORLD ORDER - Lack of Representation: - Permanent UNSC membership lopsided; major populations in South America and Africa unrepresented; populous countries like India and Brazil lack permanent seat. - Lack of Accountability: - Multilateralism skewed toward few global powers leveraging military/economic might. Examples: - US withdrawal from WHO, UNESCO, Paris Agreement. - US influence on WTO Appellate Body, rendering it defunct. - China ignoring UNCLOS ruling in South China Sea. - Credibility of WHO questioned for pro-China bias during pandemic. - MULTILATERALISM AT THE WEAKEST - Failure to Deliver: - System has failed to ensure shared benefits via integration. - Ukraine War: - UNSC failed to protect Ukraine from Russian aggression. - China’s Reluctance: - Limited cooperation on pandemic-related issues. - NEEDS OF 21ST CENTURY - Cross-National Challenges: - Issues like COVID-19 show health crises can trigger economic crises due to cross-nation supply chains and financial systems. - Global Issues: - Poverty, hunger, climate change, inequality, fake news, terrorism are cross-national; need inclusive, democratic multilateral approach. - INDIA’S VISION FOR REFORMED MULTILATERALISM - UNSC Permanent Seat: - India seeks permanent membership alongside G4 (Japan, Germany, Brazil) for true representation. - Counterterrorism: - Key priority; depoliticize sanctions process and push for Comprehensive Convention on International Terrorism (CCIT) initiated in 1996. - Rule-Based Order: - Ensure no country can arm-twist multilateral financial institutions like WTO and IMF. - Representative Order: - Lead creation of representative multilateral order engaging countries based on pluralism, democracy, liberalism; join initiatives like Alliance for Multilateralism (France & Germany). - OUR COMMON AGENDA - 75th UN Anniversary: - Member States recognized interconnected challenges needing stronger cooperation and reinvigorated multilateralism with UN at the center. - Leaders pledged to strengthen global governance and asked Secretary-General for a report with recommendations. - UN Secretary-General prepared comprehensive report “Our Common Agenda” outlining 12 action areas. - Proposed Summit of the Future to forge global consensus on shaping the future and securing it. - - - 9. UNSC REFORMS - India was elected as a non-permanent member of the United Nations Security Council with an overwhelming majority. It garnered 184 votes in the General Assembly that consists of 193 members. The two-year term will begin on 1 January 2021. - This is the eighth time India has been elected a non-permanent member of the UNSC. - WHAT IS UNSC? - The Security Council, the United Nations’ principal crisis-management body, is empowered to impose binding obligations on the 193 UN member states to maintain peace. - Few major Roles: - Peace and Security: - Ensures international peace and security. - Membership Approval: - Recommends General Assembly to accept new UN members. - Charter Changes: - Approves any changes to UN Charter. - Decision Requirement: - No charter changes or new member admissions without UNSC approval; requires agreement of all P5 members. - SECURITY COUNCIL STRUCTURE - Permanent Members: - China, France, Russia, UK, USA (P5); each has veto power. - Non-Permanent Members: - 10 rotating members by geographic region; no veto power. - Veto Origin: - Rooted in WWII; US, USSR, and UK shaped post-war order and granted vetoes to major victors. - NEED FOR UNSC REFORMS - Changing Geopolitics: - Membership and methods reflect 1945 era; global power distribution changed. - Reforms Overdue: - Only 4 non-permanent members added in 1963; overall UN membership increased from 113 to 193, UNSC composition unchanged. - Inequitable Representation: - Europe overrepresented; Asia underrepresented; Africa & South America unrepresented. - Crisis of Legitimacy: - Stalled reforms, controversial interventions in Libya and Syria question credibility. - North-South Divide: - Permanent membership shows divide; e.g., Africa has no permanent member though 75% of UNSC work relates to it. - Emerging Issues: - Economic interdependence, environmental degradation, transnational threats require consensus; UNSC critical decisions still taken by P5. - AREAS OF UNSC REFORM - Membership: - Expansion to remedy democratic deficit; debate over permanent seats & veto power. - Veto: - P5’s veto power controversial since 1945; ongoing debate about existence and use. - Regional Representation: - Expansion could include emerging powers; alternative- permanent seats for regional blocs. - Transparency: - Recent steps include more public meetings and NGO consultations; no Charter amendment needed. - UNSC & UNGA Relationship: - Improved interaction enhances transparency, accountability, legitimacy; GA consultation strengthens Council’s work. - RATIONALE FOR INDIA’S PERMANENT MEMBERSHIP - Veto Power: - To defend national interests using veto if needed. - Prestige: - Recognition of India’s global power status; key role in international peace and security. - INDIA’S CLAIMS FOR PERMANENT UNSC POSITION - Population: - Around 1/6th of global population. - Democracy: - Continuous functional democracy contributing values to UNSC. - Economy: - 5th largest economy in 2019; 3rd by PPP. - Military: - Responsible nuclear power; 3rd largest military spender. - Contributions to UN: - Largest contributor to UN Peacekeeping Operations; significant financial contributions. - Global Participation: - Active leadership in climate change, counter-terrorism, rule-based global order. - G4 Membership: - Part of G4 (Brazil, Germany, India, Japan) supporting each other’s bids. - Challenges: - Strong case but membership faces multiple challenges from various nations and factors. - 10. UN PEACEKEEPING FORCE AND ISSUES INVOLVED - A spate of attacks on United Nations Peacekeepers in the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC) amid anti-UN protests in the past few weeks has left two BSF jawans dead. The issues of growing fatalities and the lack of clear mandates for the ‘blue helmets’ were at the top of the agenda as UN Peacekeeping chief Jean-Pierre Lacroix held a meeting in Delhi as part of a tour to India, Pakistan, the UAE and Japan. - FATALITIES IN PEACEKEEPING MISSIONS - Rising Fatalities: - Malicious-act fatalities rising- 13 in 2020 → 25 in 2021 → 26 till August this year. - India’s Losses: - India lost 179 peacekeepers in 60 years; limited influence on mission types. - India’s Proposal: - 10-point plan to make those targeting UN peacekeepers more accountable. - BASIC PRINCIPLES OF UN PEACEKEEPING - Consent: - Consent of the parties involved. - Impartiality: - Neutral approach without favoring any side. - Non-Use of Force: - Force only in self-defense. - Defence of Mandate: - Protecting the assigned mission mandate. - HISTORY OF PEACEKEEPING - Origin: - Formed as a tool to maintain peace/security during Cold War when UNSC was paralyzed. - First Mission: - Arab-Israeli war; unarmed troops to monitor, report, and build confidence. - First Armed Mission: - 1956 Suez Crisis. - First Large-Scale Mission: - Congo Crisis; 20,000 troops deployed, India contributed heavily. - Total Missions: - Over 70 UN peacekeeping missions since inception. - INDIA-UN PEACEKEEPING - India is one of the founding members of United Nations. India’s commitments for maintenance of international peace have been second to none. - INDIA'S CONTRIBUTION IN PEACEKEEPING EFFORTS - Korean War: - 1950 - Medical Unit and Custodian Force for Neutral Nations Repatriation Commission. - Gaza Strip: - Indian forces served from 1956 to 1967. - Congo: - 1960 - Participation during independence from Belgium. - Other Missions: - Iran–Iraq post Gulf War I, Iraq-Kuwait border, Central America, Lebanon, Golan Heights, Rwanda, Sudan/South Sudan. - Total Troops: - 49 missions; over 2,08,000 troops; highest contribution among all countries. - Sacrifices: - 156 Indians have lost their lives; highest for any nation. - Awards: - Indian peacekeepers awarded Dag Hammarskjold award for supreme sacrifices. - All-Women Contingent: - India uniquely sent an all-women contingent in Liberia. - Training Centre: - Established in Delhi under Centre for UN Peacekeeping. - Professionalism: - Known for competence, professionalism, and rapport with local populations. - Current Deployment (2019): - 6,022 Indian troops deployed in 9 of 14 UN missions, mainly in Congo, South Sudan, Lebanon. - CHANGING NATURE OF PEACEKEEPING - Civil Wars: - Missions often in areas lacking constitutional authority. - Non-State Actors: - Sometimes involved actors not bound by norms. - Humanitarian Operations: - Monitoring relief operations. - Human Rights: - Address human rights violations. - Mine Clearance: - Assist in mine clearance operations. - Boundary Monitoring: - Monitor state boundaries. - Civilian Police Support: - Provide support to civilian police. - Infrastructure Building: - Assist in roads, railways, bridges. - Electoral Processes: - Support elections. - Reluctance of Developed Countries: - Less contribution of troops. - Fund Misappropriation: - Allegations by contributing nations of misuse of funds. - Foreign Exchange Motive: - Some countries (Pakistan, Bangladesh) view peacekeeping as revenue source, acting irresponsibly. - INDIA’S NEW TEN-POINT PLAN FOR PEACEKEEPING - 1. Clear and realistic mandates matched with adequate resources; troop-contributing countries need a role in decision-making. - 2. Avoid misleading terminologies; deploy missions prudently with recognition of limitations. - 3. Coordinate with host governments to counter misinformation and bring perpetrators of crimes against peacekeepers to justice. - 4. Ensure urgent installation of memorial wall for fallen peacekeepers at UN headquarters. - 5. Establish trust and coordination between mission leadership and host state for operational success. - 6. Emphasize the role of women peacekeepers; first all-women contingent deployed in Liberia (2007). - 7. Introduce advanced technology; supported "Unite Aware" platform in 2021 and signed MoU with UNC4ISR Academy in Uganda. - 8. Address insecurity of civilians caused by terrorist groups; primary responsibility lies with host government. - 9. Regional approach for armed conflict resolution and collective security; involve regional/sub-regional organizations in mediation, monitoring ceasefires, peace accords, and post-conflict rebuilding. - 10. Factor in exit strategies from inception to avoid redundant missions draining UN resources. - 11. G7 - NEED FOR EXPANSION - The Grouping began as the Group of Six in 1975- with a meeting of leaders of US, UK, West Germany, Italy, France and Japan, because of the Oil crisis and global currency exchange issues that decided to meet annually. - The Group of 7 (G7) is an informal group of seven countries — the United States, Canada, France, Germany, Italy, Japan and the United Kingdom, the heads of which hold an annual summit with European Union and other invitees. - Together the member countries represent 40% of global GDP and 10% of the world’s population. - G7 has no legal existence, permanent secretariat or official members. - Leaders of G7 countries meets annually and discusses emerging global issues. - India has been a special invitee to the G-7 on several occasions in past two decades- including 5 consecutive years from 2005-2009 during global economic crisis. This year Indian PM was invited at summit. - KEY OUTCOMES OF G7 MEET - China Approach: - United approach to diversify supply chains to reduce dependence on China; not decoupling but de-risking. - China-Taiwan Tensions: - Allies agreed on response if China acts unilaterally against Taiwan. - Ukraine Support: - PM Modi assured Ukraine of India’s support to resolve the war. - Hiroshima Action Statement: - Launch for resilient global food security with partner countries. - Infrastructure Investment: - Mobilize $600 billion for quality infrastructure via PGII. - Indo-Pacific Commitment: - Free, open, inclusive, prosperous, and secure region based on rule of law and human rights. - Russia Condemnation: - Condemned Russia’s war against Ukraine as violation of international law. - Nuclear Disarmament: - Commitment to a world without nuclear weapons with security for all. - CHALLENGES FACED BY G7 GROUPING - - Changing Economic Situation: - G7’s share of global GDP has fallen from two-thirds to less than a third (PPP) and less than half (MER). - Emerging Economies: - E7 (Brazil, China, India, Indonesia, Mexico, Russia, Turkey) account for over a third of global GDP (PPP). - India’s Growth Projection: - India’s GDP projected at $17 trillion by 2030 and $42 trillion by 2050 (PPP), second after China. - LIMITATIONS OF G7 - Elite Club: - Membership excludes emerging economies like India and China. - Polarisation: - Excluding Russia and China deepens global polarization. - Military Focus: - Economic agenda often overshadowed by political/military issues. - Economic Failure: - Failed to prevent 2007-08 economic downturn; led to G20 rise. - Contemporary Issues: - Lacked focus on COVID-19, climate change, Daesh, state collapse in West Asia. - Fossil Fuel Phase-Out: - No concrete plan despite prior announcements. - Lack of Formal Structure: - No charter or secretariat. - Lack of Representation: - Excludes major global players like Russia, China, India. - US Challenges: - US leadership questioned G7 unity under Trump. - China Threat: - Seen as economic, ideological, and geopolitical challenge. - Countering BRI: - Partnership for Global Infrastructure and Investment (PGII) announced. - WHAT IT MEANS FOR INDIA - Diplomatic Balancing: - Tightrope between West and Russia-China becomes more complex. - Economic Initiatives: - India joined IPEF; yet to join PGII; US-China rivalry in Indo-Pacific intensifies. - Partnerships: - Strengthened ties with Indonesia, Brazil, South Africa, ASEAN for balanced engagement. - Balancing Role: - India avoids anti-West language at BRICS summit and moderates condemnations at G7 outreach. - WHY WE NEED A NEW INSTITUTION - Global Challenges: - Mechanism needed for trade wars, economic slowdown, political turmoil, counterterrorism, and non-proliferation. - Inclusive Membership: - Include seven future leading economies plus Germany, Japan, UK, France, Mexico, Turkey, South Korea, Australia. - Specific Issues: - Address Ukraine war, global economic slowdown, Iran’s nuclear program, peace in Afghanistan, Gulf/West Asia stability, tensions in Korean peninsula and South China Sea. - 12. G20 AS AN ALTERNATIVE TO G7 - Power and prestige of Group of Twenty (G20), a forum for finance ministers and central bank governors from nineteen of world's largest countries and EU, has surpassed that of G7. - Emerging powers including Brazil, China, India, Mexico, and South Africa, whose absence from G7 was often noted, all belong to G20.
